## T3. Generative models and classically hard sampling

### I-20261012-03: Trainable generative quantum models on hard-to-sample distributions

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | external batch A |
| Hypothesis | A generative quantum model learns to sample a classically intractable distribution from polynomial data, while no classical generative model of comparable size matches its sample quality. |
| Mechanism | Huang et al. introduce generative quantum models that are hard to simulate classically, have no barren plateaus and are efficiently trainable with local costs. |
| Data | Classical samples from a classically hard target (random IQP circuit output). |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | 6-qubit IQP target, 1000 training samples; QCBM with the architecture of arXiv:2509.09033; baselines: RBM, normalizing flow, KDE; metric: total variation distance; success: quantum TV<=0.05 with <=1e3 samples while classical needs >=1e5 or plateaus >0.1. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | At 6 qubits the target is not hard; the 68-qubit demonstration is beyond simulation. |
| References cited | 2509.09033, 2010.02174 |
| Reference checks | 2509.09033: **OK** (Exists (Huang, Broughton, Eassa, Neven, Babbush, McClean). The abstract matches the mechanism sentence: hard to simulate, efficiently trainable, no barren plateaus or local-minima proliferation. But it is misapplied. The IDQNN is a conditional model p(y\|x), trained by a closed-form local estimator that needs inputs with x_j=0 and all neighbours 1. App. C.2.a says x=0^n data 'provides no useful information' for learning. The paper also says classical models 'are accurate at very small sizes'.); 2010.02174: **CLAIM NOT IN PAPER** (Exists (Liu, Arunachalam, Temme: 'A rigorous and robust quantum speed-up in supervised machine learning'). It is a supervised-classification separation (discrete-log kernel, fault-tolerant). It says nothing about generative modelling or sampling hardness. The relevant generative separation is Sweke et al., arXiv:2007.14451 (DDH-based).) |
| Technical problems | The success rule is beaten by a lookup table. On 5 random 6-qubit IQP instances (Porter-Thomas-like, 2^n*sum p^2 = 1.86), the raw empirical histogram reaches TV = 0.091 at 1e3 samples, 0.045 at 4e3 and 0.028 at 1e4 (our simulation). So 'classical needs >= 1e5 or plateaus > 0.1' is false by construction. The uniform distribution already has TV = 0.34.; 'QCBM with the architecture of arXiv:2509.09033' on an unconditional IQP target is undefined. The 2509.09033 training uses conditional inputs with local decoupling, and x=0^n-only data cannot identify the parameters.; A gradient-trained, well-specified IQP Born machine does not reach the claimed TV. In our scratch pilot (exact NLL, 12 random restarts, 600 Adam steps), it got KL 0.10-0.14 at n=6, N=1000, against 0.023-0.030 for an add-alpha lookup table. At n=8 and n=10 it stayed near uniform-level KL. This is consistent with barren plateaus under full-range initialisation (arXiv:2603.14576).; Normalizing flows are continuous-density models. They are not a natural baseline for 6-bit strings, and KDE on bitstrings is essentially a smoothed histogram. The strongest baselines are missing: autoregressive chains, MPS Born machines and the lookup table.; At 6 qubits the target is classically easy (the card admits this). Any 'win' tests well-specification, not quantumness, and a classical simulation of the same parametric family reproduces it exactly. |
| Kill-criteria check | K6, K4 |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2509.09033 (The IDQNN generative-advantage proposal. Its baselines are untuned; classical models are accurate at small sizes.); arXiv:1904.02214 (Ising (IQP-type) Born machine with hardness claims and trained losses, compared with a classical model.); arXiv:2503.02934 (Classically trained IQP Born machines up to 1000 qubits, compared with simple energy-based models.); arXiv:2603.14576 (Barren plateaus of MMD-trained IQP-QCBMs under random full-angle initialisation.); arXiv:2110.05517 (Statistical-query hardness of learning local-circuit output distributions, for classical and quantum learners alike.) |
| Status | **MERGED into I-20261012-31** |
| Decision note | Same mechanism as I-20261012-31: learn small instances of the 2509.09033 circuit family with a Born machine and compare with classical generators. Its own minimal test is ill posed. A lookup table defeats its success rule at 6 qubits. The 2509.09033 estimator cannot learn from x=0^n (unconditional IQP) data. The claimed sampling hardness does not exist at 6 qubits (K6). The baselines (RBM, normalizing flow, KDE) omit the strongest ones (K4). The idea's useful core survives only as part of I-20261012-31's best version. |

### I-20261012-11: Matchgate quantum circuit Born machine for classically hard parity distributions

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | external batch B |
| Hypothesis | A matchgate-based QCBM outperforms classical GANs at modelling complex parity distributions. |
| Mechanism | Claims matchgate circuits generate distributions that are classically hard to sample or evaluate due to fermionic sign structure. |
| Data | Classical bitstrings from a hard parity-check distribution. |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | 8-qubit matchgate QCBM vs tuned MLP-GAN; metrics: MMD and NLL; success: lower MMD with fewer parameters. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | Autoregressive models may approximate the target. |
| References cited | 2509.09033 |
| Reference checks | 2509.09033: **CLAIM NOT IN PAPER** (The IDQNN uses RZ and CZ gates with X measurement. No matchgate, free-fermion or 'fermionic sign structure' content appeared in the pages retrieved (abstract, Sec. II-III, App. C, H.2, H.4). The paper does not support the matchgate-hardness claim.) |
| Technical problems | Nearest-neighbour matchgate circuits are classically efficiently simulable, including sampling and marginals (Terhal & DiVincenzo, quant-ph/0108010; Jozsa & Miyake, 0804.4050). The 'fermionic sign structure' of non-interacting fermions does not create sampling hardness. Conjectured hardness requires non-Gaussian (magic) input states, as in arXiv:2511.13844.; The uniform distribution over a parity-check (linear) code is learnable from O(n) samples by Gaussian elimination. It can be sampled exactly by random linear combinations of a basis. With noise (LPN), it is conjecturally hard for quantum learners too, so there is no quantum-classical separation either way.; A single MLP-GAN baseline is a weak, notoriously unstable model for discrete data (K4). The strong baselines are missing: autoregressive chains, MPS, the lookup table, and Gaussian elimination for parity structure.; 'Lower MMD with fewer parameters' is not an advantage criterion. DESIGN.md §6 compares tuned performance, and low-order MMD cannot detect high-order (parity) structure (arXiv:2305.02881). |
| Kill-criteria check | K3, K4, K7 |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2511.13844 (Fermionic Born Machines: magic states plus matchgate (FLO) circuits, trained classically. Hardness comes from the magic inputs.); arXiv:2608.31117 (Benchmarked magic and passive FBMs against likelihood-trained classical models. Moment-trained FBMs generalise poorly.); arXiv:2605.10258 (Parity supervision for IQP Born machines. A circuit-free classical spectral reconstruction already carries the generalisation.); arXiv:2511.01845 (Classical surrogation conditions for QCBMs, including matchcircuit ansatze.); quant-ph/0108010 (Classical simulation of non-interacting-fermion (matchgate) circuits.) |
| Status | **REJECTED** |
| Decision note | K3: matchgate (free-fermion) circuits with product inputs and computational-basis measurement are efficiently classically simulable. The model is easy to train exactly because it is classically simulable, and there is no hardness to exploit. Hardness needs magic inputs, which is the existing Fermionic Born Machine (2511.13844, K7). The evidence would be a single untuned MLP-GAN comparison (K4), and the 'hard parity distribution' target is classically easy to learn and sample. |

### I-20261012-23: Born machine generalisation beyond moment matching

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | external batch C |
| Hypothesis | A QCBM trained with a support-aware objective generalises to unseen valid patterns better than tuned autoregressive, RBM and tensor-network generators on structured distributions whose large-scale sampling is hard. |
| Mechanism | Circuits naturally sample; support-aware objective discourages memorising observed examples. |
| Data | Classical samples from structured targets (small circuit outputs, cardinality-constrained supports). |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | 8-10 qubits, exact target probabilities; vs MMD-trained QCBM, autoregressive, RBM, tensor networks at equal samples; metrics: exact TV, held-out support coverage, calibration; success: lower held-out error than best classical on several families. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | Classical models reproduce small distributions; support knowledge may bias the task. |
| References cited | 2509.09033, 2608.31117 |
| Reference checks | 2509.09033: **OK** (Exists and motivates hard-sampling circuit families. It says nothing about support-aware objectives or generalisation on structured (cardinality) data.); 2608.31117: **OK** (Exists (Raj, Mathur, Perdomo-Ortiz). It calls for coverage-aware losses as future work, but its evidence goes against this hypothesis. Moment-trained IQP and FBM models generalise worse than likelihood-trained classical models. The only good quantum model wins by hard-coding the support constraint. Theorem 1: moment losses of capacity d have exact minimisers on <= d+1 strings.) |
| Technical problems | Cardinality-constrained targets (uniform over fixed-Hamming-weight strings) are trivially easy to sample classically. This contradicts the hypothesis's 'structured distributions whose large-scale sampling is hard'.; Knowing the support makes the task solvable by hard-coding it. The passive FBM in 2608.31117 wins exactly this way, and a classical generator restricted to the valid set does the same.; The scalable (classically trainable) QCBM losses are moment losses with provably sparse minimisers (2608.31117, Theorem 1). Exact-likelihood training of a QCBM is only available in simulation at small n. At small n the classical likelihood models are already strong, so the quantum side has no scalable training route that the classical side lacks.; No reason is given why a quantum circuit with a support-aware loss would generalise better than a classical generator with the same loss. This is the K1 pattern (problem-agnostic circuit, classical imitation possible). |
| Kill-criteria check | K7, K1 |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2608.31117 (13-model benchmark up to 30 qubits. Likelihood-trained classical models generalise better than moment-trained IQP and FBM models.); arXiv:2303.15626 (QCBM vs Transformers, RNNs, VAEs and WGANs on 20-bit cardinality data. Claims a QCBM edge in the data-limited regime.); arXiv:2207.13645 (12-qubit QCBM generalisation on cardinality-constrained data.); arXiv:2201.08770 (Generalisation metrics. Tensor-network models beat GANs.); arXiv:2605.10258 (Generalisation in IQP Born machines via parity supervision, reproduced by a circuit-free spectral method.) |
| Status | **REJECTED** |
| Decision note | K7: QCBM-vs-classical generalisation benchmarks on cardinality-constrained data exist (2201.08770, 2207.13645, 2303.15626). The 2026 study 2608.31117 shows, up to 30 qubits, that likelihood-trained classical models (Transformers, RNNs, TNBM) beat moment-trained quantum ones. K1 in spirit: a 'support-aware objective' is a loss design that any classical generator can use, so nothing in the mechanism is quantum. Its circuit-output family duplicates I-20261012-31. |

### I-20261012-31: Learning small instances of classically hard quantum output distributions

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | external batch D |
| Hypothesis | A quantum Born machine beats tuned autoregressive and tensor-network models at learning small instances of a quantum-circuit output distribution from limited samples. |
| Mechanism | The target family is motivated by hard sampling; at small sizes this is a sample-efficiency hypothesis. |
| Data | Classical bitstrings from the circuit family of arXiv:2509.09033, 4-10 qubits. |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | Exact target probabilities; Born machine vs autoregressive, MPS and GBT-based generators at matched samples and parameters; held-out NLL and TV; success: consistent quantum improvement. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | Small distributions are tractable and memorisable. |
| References cited | 2509.09033 |
| Reference checks | 2509.09033: **OK** (Exists. The family (IDQNN, Def. 16), the training set D(x) (App. C.2.a) and the closed-form estimator eq. S.2.1 are as described. The paper itself says classical seq2seq models 'are accurate at very small sizes' and that its XGBoost baseline used one fixed configuration.) |
| Technical problems | At <= 10 shallow qubits the IDQNN is a deep circuit of <= 5 qubits and depth <= 5, which is trivially simulable. The hypothesis can only be about inductive bias or sample efficiency, not hardness.; If the Born machine is the target's own family, a win comes from well-specification. The classical twin (same family, simulated classically) reproduces it exactly, so it is not evidence. The test model must be structure-agnostic.; 'Matched samples and parameters' handicaps the classical side. Classical baselines must be tuned over capacity with at least as many configurations (DESIGN.md §6, condition 2), not capped at the quantum parameter count.; The lookup-table baseline is missing, and it dominates in this regime. In our scratch pilot on random IQP targets, the tuned add-alpha histogram had the lowest KL at n=6 (N=1000: 0.023-0.030), at n=8 (N=1000: 0.09-0.13 vs >= 0.22 for the HEA Born machine, MPS, autoregressive chain and gradient-trained IQP; N=1e4: 0.012-0.013 vs MPS 0.021-0.045) and at n=10 (N=3000: 0.12 vs >= 0.30).; The paper's estimator (eq. S.2.1/S.4.14) is sign-blind. P(y_j=1) is even in theta_j, so it recovers \|theta_j\| only. Flipping one angle's sign equals XOR-flipping the outputs of that qubit's neighbours at x=0^n (exact identity, checked numerically), at TV 0.50 from the original. In our pilot with theta ~ U[0, 2pi), the sign-blind estimator scored KL 0.55-0.93 (worse than uniform, 0.20-0.34). With signs resolved it scored 0.04-0.06 at N=1000 and 0.001 at N=3e4. A replication must either restrict beta to [0, pi/2] (whether the paper does is NOT VERIFIED) or resolve relative signs. Pairwise-decoupled inputs can do this, since <X_j X_k> changes sign under one flip (our derivation). |
| Kill-criteria check | K6, K7 |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2509.09033 (The source family and claim. Untuned XGBoost and transformer baselines; classical models are accurate at small sizes.); arXiv:2110.05517 (Learning local-circuit output distributions is SQ-hard for any learner. Clifford outputs are classically learnable from samples.); arXiv:2207.03140 (One T gate makes distribution learning hard. Simulability does not imply learnability.); arXiv:2512.24801 (Anticoncentrated targets give exponentially concentrated losses, so they are untrainable on average.); arXiv:2609.28802 (Sampling hardness of an unknown source cannot be certified from polynomially many samples. This concerns ordinary datasets, not promised families.); arXiv:2605.28986 (Classical EBM learnability of quantum measurement data tracks simulation-hardness measures.) |
| Status | **SHORTLISTED (weakened in adversarial review: apply the required fixes before building)** |
| Decision note | Shortlisted only as a killing experiment. At 4-10 qubits there is no sampling hardness (K6 for the hardness claim), but the inductive-bias question is testable and cheap. As stated it is not decisive: a target-matched Born machine wins by well-specification, and its classical twin reproduces that exactly. The sharpened version is the theme's best version. The decisive model is a structure-agnostic Born machine, with matched-structure and classical-control arms. It also checks 2509.09033's small-size claims with tuned baselines (K7: adds the tuned baselines and controls the original lacks). |
| Decisive test | IDQNN targets (2509.09033, 2x5 and 3x3 grids, the paper's D(x) mixture, inference at x=0^n) and random degree-2 IQP targets at n=10, with N in {1000, 10000} and 5 seeds. A structure-agnostic Born machine (hardware-efficient, exact NLL; and IQP-MMD) faces the best tuned classical generator: add-alpha lookup table, tuned HistGradientBoosting, logistic and MLP autoregressive chains, MPS Born machine with chi up to 32, and RBM. Metric: exact KL. Decided by the DESIGN.md §6 rule, with a random Ising control family and structure-aware quantum and classical twins reported alongside. A win counts only if it holds on the quantum targets and not on the control. |

### T3 best version: Well-specified or quantum? A matched-control generative benchmark on IDQNN and IQP targets at 6-10 qubits

*Kind:* killing experiment. *Built from:* I-20261012-31, I-20261012-03.

- **Hypothesis:** Pre-registered prediction, which the test can refute. On exactly simulated quantum-circuit targets at 6-10 qubits, no structure-agnostic quantum Born machine beats the best tuned classical generator in exact KL at the same sample size. The targets are the IDQNN of arXiv:2509.09033 queried at x=0^n, and random degree-2 IQP circuits. The classical generators are the add-alpha lookup table, tuned autoregressive chains and an MPS Born machine. Only models given the target's structure beat the classical generators, and their classical twins match them exactly.
- **Mechanism:** A small-scale generative 'quantum win' can have three sources. (i) Well-specification: the model family contains the target. A classical simulation of the same family reproduces this. (ii) The estimator or trainability: in our pilot, 2509.09033's closed-form local estimator works once angle signs are resolved, while gradient MLE of the same well-specified family fails. (iii) A quantum-native inductive bias of a structure-agnostic circuit for circuit-generated data. Only (iii) could be specific to quantum models at this scale. Sampling hardness itself cannot be probed at <= 10 qubits: 2509.09033 says classical models are accurate at very small sizes, and 2609.28802 shows source hardness cannot be certified from samples. The experiment therefore isolates (iii), using arms that measure (i) and (ii) and a classical-target control that tests specificity.
- **Data:** Three target families. (a) IDQNN (2509.09033, Def. 16) on 2x3, 2x4, 2x5 and 3x3 grids. RZ angles are drawn from U[0, pi], so the paper's estimator is identifiable; the sign-blind U[0, 2pi) variant is a secondary arm. Training inputs follow the paper's D(x): 1/3 x=0^n, 1/3 i.i.d. P(0)=0.6, 1/3 i.i.d. P(0)=0.2. Evaluation is on p(y|x=0^n). (b) Random all-to-all degree-2 IQP with angles U[0, 2pi), n in {6, 8, 10}; these are anticoncentrated, with 2^n*sum p^2 of about 2 measured. (c) Control: random Ising/Boltzmann distributions on the same n with matched Shannon entropy. Total samples N in {1000, 3000, 10000}, 5 seeds (seed = target instance + data draw). Exact target probabilities come from enumerating 2^n <= 1024 strings.
- **Quantum model:** Test models, both structure-agnostic and exactly simulated. Q-HEA: RY-RZ layers plus a CZ ring, depth L in {2, 4, 6, 8}, 3 initialisations per configuration. It is trained by exact NLL with Adam, early-stopped on a 20% validation split. Its conditional variant for IDQNN prepares |0> or |+> from x, as the IDQNN does. Q-IQP-MMD: an all-to-all IQP Born machine trained with Gaussian-kernel MMD (bandwidth grid, data-dependent initialisation), as in 2503.02934. It is structure-agnostic on IDQNN and Ising targets and well-specified on IQP. Reference arms, not counted as evidence: Q-IDQNN uses the paper's closed-form estimator plus relative-sign resolution from pairwise-decoupled inputs, and is also run sign-blind; Q-IQP-NLL is the well-specified IQP trained by gradient NLL.
- **Classical baselines:** All baselines are tuned on the same folds by held-out NLL, with configuration counts at least those of the quantum arms. C-hist: add-alpha lookup table on the x=0^n samples (alpha in {0.01 ... 10}). C-chain: autoregressive per-bit classifiers conditioned on x and earlier y bits. These are HistGradientBoosting (DESIGN.md §4 axes, reduced grid), logistic regression with pairwise features (C grid), and MLP (hidden layers x alpha grid). The paper's untuned XGBoost setting (100 trees, lr 0.3, depth 6) is replicated with HistGradientBoosting as a reference. C-MPS: MPS Born machine, chi in {2, 4, 8, 16, 32}, exact NLL. C-RBM: hidden units in {8, 16, 32}, exact partition function by enumeration. C-twin: the structure-aware classical twin, with the same estimator and exact classical sampling. It is identical to Q-IDQNN at this size and is reported to make that explicit.
- **Access model:** Classical data only. Every model gets the same N (x, y) samples. There are no quantum states, copies or coherent access. All quantum models are classically simulated. At scale, the only quantum resource would be the sampler at inference. There is no physical-access asymmetry, so this is not an M8-type separation. The structure-aware arms (Q-IDQNN, C-twin, Q-IQP on IQP) are told the target family; the test arms (Q-HEA, Q-IQP-MMD on non-IQP targets) and the classical baselines are not.
- **Metric:** Primary: exact forward KL(p(.|0^n) || q(.|0^n)) for IDQNN, and the unconditional KL for IQP and Ising, computed by enumeration. Secondary: TV, linear XEB fidelity, held-out NLL, and the number of samples needed to reach KL <= 0.05.
- **Success rule:** Per (family, n, N) cell over 5 seeds, using DESIGN.md §6. A model beats the best tuned classical model if its KL is lower on >= 4/5 seeds and the mean paired difference exceeds the SD of the paired differences, with the classical side having at least as many configurations. A quantum-native inductive bias is declared only if Q-HEA or Q-IQP-MMD beats the best classical model in >= 2 of the 4 primary quantum-target cells, and does not beat it in the matching Ising-control cells. The primary cells are IDQNN 2x5 and IQP n=10, each at N in {1000, 10000}. Any other outcome is recorded as 'no quantum-native generative inductive bias at <= 10 qubits'. Wins by the structure-aware arms are reported as well-specification effects and never counted. Secondary checks, pre-registered: (a) whether the tuned C-chain beats the paper's untuned XGBoost-style chain on the IDQNN cells; (b) the KL of the sign-blind vs sign-resolved estimator on U[0, 2pi) IDQNN targets.
- **Milestone:** M4 (trainable-and-not-simulable generative models; FIELD_MAP open problem 3 asks for independent replication of arXiv:2509.09033). Secondarily M2 (do quantum-generated data favour quantum models).
- **Kill check:** Partly done as a scratch pilot (3 seeds, small grids). On random IQP targets the tuned lookup table beat every model at n=6, 8 and 10 for N <= 1e4: n=8, N=1000: 0.09-0.13 vs >= 0.22; n=10, N=3000: 0.12 vs >= 0.30. The gradient-trained well-specified IQP stayed near uniform-level KL at n >= 8. On IDQNN 2x3 to 2x5 targets, the sign-resolved estimator reached KL 0.04-0.06 at N=1000, against 0.08-0.30 for the lookup table. The sign-blind estimator scored 0.55-0.93, worse than uniform. First step of the full run: repeat the IQP and IDQNN n=10 cells with the full grids. If Q-HEA and Q-IQP-MMD lose to the lookup table or MPS on >= 4/5 seeds in every quantum-target cell, stop and record the negative result.
- **Cost estimate:** New module src/qmlrc/generative.py, about 550 lines. It holds the targets (IDQNN with conditional sampling, IQP, Ising, with exact probability vectors), the models (lookup table, chains, MPS, RBM, HEA and IQP Born machines in JAX, IDQNN estimator with sign resolution) and the metrics. Also about +150 lines in experiments.py and about 150 lines of tests. The tests cover: Q-HEA probabilities against PennyLane; MPS against brute force; the sign-flip identity p_{-theta_j}(y) = p_theta(y XOR e_{N(j)}); and jitted vs eager outputs. That last test is needed because jitted fancy-indexing of closed-over int64 arrays returned wrong IQP phases in jax 0.7.2 in our pilot; the repository's VQC was checked and is unaffected. CPU: the scratch pilot took 55-130 s per (n, N, seed) for 5 model families with small grids. The full grids should take about 2-4 min per cell-seed at n=10. The primary cells (3 families x 2 N x 5 seeds) need about 60-120 CPU-minutes, and the n in {6, 8} sweep about the same again. Run as 6 experiments of <= 10 min wall-clock each on 4 cores.
- **Win teaches:** Suppose a structure-agnostic Born machine beats tuned lookup tables, chains and MPS on circuit-generated targets but not on Ising controls. That would be the first evidence in this repository of a quantum-native generative inductive bias, and would justify a scaling study (n = 12-20 with tensor-network-simulated targets) under M4/M2. It would still say nothing about classical sampling hardness, which no simulated experiment can show.
- **Loss teaches:** Small-scale generative 'quantum wins' on circuit targets would then come from well-specification or weak baselines. Those are reproduced by a classical twin, a lookup table or an MPS. The only generative-advantage route left would be 2509.09033's promise-family sampling advantage, which cannot be tested on a laptop. T3 would then be closed for this repository until an idea brings a new testable mechanism. Side results: whether 2509.09033's small-size baselines were under-tuned, and whether its estimator needs an explicit sign-resolution step.
- **Closest prior work:** arXiv:2509.09033 (IDQNN generative advantage. Classical training, quantum inference, untuned XGBoost and transformer baselines.); arXiv:2303.15626 (QCBM vs tuned classical generators on cardinality data. Claims a data-limited QCBM edge.); arXiv:2608.31117 (Likelihood-trained classical models beat moment-trained quantum generators on generalisation, up to 30 qubits.); arXiv:2503.02934 (Classically trained IQP Born machines vs simple energy-based models.); arXiv:2603.14576 (Barren plateaus of MMD-trained IQP Born machines under random full-angle initialisation.); arXiv:2110.05517 (SQ-hardness of learning local-circuit output distributions for any learner.); arXiv:2609.28802 (Source sampling hardness cannot be certified from polynomially many samples.); arXiv:2101.08354 (Unconditional expressivity separation, Bayesian networks vs their quantum extension. The strongest prior reason to expect a quantum-native bias.)

**Adversarial review: WEAKENED.** WEAKENED. The experiment is feasible for unconditional models and not already done in this form. As designed, though, it cannot isolate a 'quantum-native generative inductive bias'. Main problems: (1) A false positive is possible. At x=0^n the IDQNN of 2509.09033 (Def. 16, verified in the full text) is exactly a degree-2 IQP circuit; I reproduced it with an all-to-all IQP to within 1e-17. So Q-IQP-MMD, counted as a structure-agnostic test arm, is well-specified on the IDQNN primary cells, and its wins there would be wrongly declared a quantum-native bias. (2) The Ising control is exactly contained in the classical baselines, so the specificity test can never fail. (3) In the cells with N >= 2^n, the tuned lookup table is near the (K-1)/(2N) rate (IQP n=10: 0.236 / 0.116 / 0.045 KL at N = 1e3 / 3e3 / 1e4). A structure-agnostic model cannot win there unless effectively well-specified, so the null result is nearly forced and uninformative. (4) At 10 qubits or fewer every Born machine is a complex MPS with chi <= 32, so even a Q-HEA win would show a parameterisation bias, not a quantum one. The classical MPS must be complex and properly trained; the pilot's real MPS was under-trained. Smaller problems: MMD training vs KL scoring confounds the result; the conditional Q-HEA leaks the IDQNN input encoding and costs about 10x the budgeted time (measured 0.48 s per step); the sign-resolved IDQNN result used oracle signs, although a data-driven maximum-likelihood sign search reproduces it. The fixes are concrete: reclassify Q-IQP on IDQNN as structure-aware, use controls misspecified for both sides, add N in {100, 300} and N=infinity arms, train by NLL, add complex-MPS and max-ent baselines, and reword the claims. With them it can be shortlisted as a killing experiment, partly a replication. Scratch checks are in /tmp/claude-0/-home-user-quantum-ml-reality-check/3242e8f8-b193-546c-b381-3fe888e7979d/scratchpad/round01/t3_review/: check1.py (IDQNN equals IQP, sign search), check2.py (lookup-table floors), time_hea.py (runtime). I used arxiv_search.py 3 times and resolved IDs with curl twice. 2110.05517 was not re-verified in this review.

- *Already done:* Not done in this exact form, so K7 does not kill it. But each piece has been run before, and the result is largely predictable. (1) Classical learners trained on quantum-circuit samples at this scale. 2605.28986 (full text read): a deep energy-based model with exact partition function, trained by maximum likelihood on samples of 10-qubit random MPS and Clifford+T circuits; learnability tracks simulation cost. 2010.11983 (abstract only; Niu et al., resolved by arXiv API): DBM, GAN and LSTM models trained on samples of deep random circuits. (2) Whether a quantum ansatz has a bias for a given data family. 2511.01845 (alphaXiv report only): IQP, matchgate, Heisenberg and Haldane ansätze across datasets at n=6. The IQP ansatz showed no specialised bias. The paper also studies MPS and Pauli-propagation surrogates. (3) IQP-generated targets fitted by IQP learners. 2603.06440 (report only): IQP learners on IQP-generated targets at 16 qubits, and an IQP model against RBM/DCGAN on turbulence data. (4) Quantum vs tuned classical generators on classical data. 2303.15626 (resolved by arXiv API; abstract level). 2608.31117 (report only): likelihood-trained classical models generalise better than MMD-trained IQP and fermionic Born machines, up to 30 qubits. (5) 2509.09033 itself (full text, pp. 4, 7, 17-21, 42, 47-48): classical models are "accurate at very small sizes" (C.2.b, p. 19; Fig. 2 discussion, p. 7). So a test at 10 qubits or fewer cannot probe the paper's claim, and the secondary check (a) tells us little. That check compares a tuned chain against the paper's untuned XGBoost chain (n_estimators=100, eta=0.3, max_depth=6; verified on p. 19). (6) The 'quantum-native' rationale borrowed from 2101.08354 (full text pages read). Its quantum extension (BBQC) is an MPS/tensor network, and the paper notes the separation is realised by classically simulable MPS (numerics vs HMMs on real data). So the cited bias is already reproducible classically. What is new is the matched-control design (structure-aware vs structure-agnostic arms, a lookup-table baseline, the paired decision rule) on IDQNN and IQP targets. That is enough to shortlist it as a killing experiment, but only once the design flaws below are fixed.
- *Classical can match:* Yes. At n=10 and N >= ~2^n, the tuned add-alpha lookup table already sits close to the (K-1)/(2N) rate. My check (check2.py, 5 instances each): - Random IQP, n=10: lookup-table KL 0.236 / 0.116 / 0.045 at N = 1e3 / 3e3 / 1e4. Uniform is 0.41-0.44. A well-specified model would reach d/(2N) = 0.028 / 0.009 / 0.003 (d=55). - IDQNN 2x5 (only N/3 samples at x=0^n): 0.295 / 0.223 / 0.111. So only a model whose family effectively contains the target can win. A structure-agnostic shallow HEA cannot fit a Porter-Thomas random IQP state. Baselines the proposal forgot or under-specifies: (a) A complex-valued MPS Born machine trained by two-site DMRG-style sweeps. Any Born machine on 10 qubits is exactly a complex MPS with bond dimension chi <= 32, and the 2x5 IDQNN target is a chi=4 complex MPS. The pilot's MPS is real-valued and trained by plain Adam. It ended worse than uniform on one cell (n=10, N=3000, seed 2: KL 0.64 vs 0.42), so it is under-trained, which is a K4 problem on the classical side. (b) A fully visible Boltzmann / max-ent model with 2- and 3-body terms and exact partition function, plus a correlator (Walsh-coefficient) max-ent surrogate that matches the same moments Q-IQP-MMD matches, in the style of 2511.01845. This is the natural classical twin of moment-trained IQP. (c) For IDQNN: maximum likelihood over the 2^n sign patterns of |theta_hat|, using only the x=0^n samples. On 2x5 at N=1000 it gives exactly the oracle-sign KL on all 5 seeds (0.016-0.043; sign-blind 0.07-0.74). The 'sign-resolution' arm is therefore a two-line classical fix, not a scientific finding. Most importantly, at <= 10 qubits a Q-HEA 'win' over a real MPS could come from complex amplitudes and interference. A complex MPS, or the same circuit simulated classically, has both. The experiment can therefore detect at most a circuit-parameterisation or geometry bias, never a quantum-native one.
- *Access model issue:* No physical-access asymmetry. Everything is classical data from the same N samples and all quantum models are simulated, so this is correctly labelled as not an M8 separation. There is an information-access leak, though. The conditional Q-HEA 'prepares |0> or |+> from x, as the IDQNN does'. That input map is exactly Def. 16's mechanism (0 -> |+>, 1 -> |0>, so qubits with x_i=1 decouple), so the 'structure-agnostic' quantum arm is handed target structure the classical chains must learn. Either remove the map (train Q-HEA unconditionally on the x=0^n samples) or give the classical chains the same hint as a feature. A minor asymmetry: C-hist uses only the ~N/3 samples at x=0^n, while the chains and the structure-aware arms use all N. Report this explicitly.
- *Feasibility:* Partly feasible. - Unconditional models at n=10 are cheap; the pilot took 55-130 s per cell-seed. - The conditional Q-HEA is not cheap. I measured 0.48 s per Adam step at L=8, batch 256 (repository venv, 4 threads), and 75 ms at batch 32. With N=1e4, ~600 steps and 12 configurations (4 depths x 3 inits), that is about 35-60 minutes per cell-seed, roughly 10x the stated 2-4 minutes. The single-core pinning required by DESIGN.md §5 would make it slower still. - The full plan is about 12 experiments, not 6: the n in {6,8} sweep, the 3x3 grid, the sign-blind arm, Q-IQP-NLL, HGB/MLP chains on 10 bits with CV grids, and RBMs. Decisiveness is weak: (1) Q-IQP-MMD is well-specified on IDQNN, so the rule allows a false positive (see technical errors). (2) The Ising control is classically well-specified (a pairwise log-linear model or RBM contains it exactly), so 'does not beat it on Ising' is satisfied automatically and tests no specificity. Matching Shannon entropy is the wrong matching variable. (3) In the regime N >= 2^n the null result is nearly forced, so it cannot tell 'no bias' apart from 'regime where no bias could show'. (4) MMD-trained models scored on forward KL lose through objective mismatch (2608.31117, Theorem 1 at report level), not through lack of inductive bias. (5) A Q-HEA loss mixes up trainability (K3) with inductive bias, because there is no N=infinity (exact-target) fit to measure approximation error. (6) The conclusion 'no quantum-native generative inductive bias at <= 10 qubits' over-generalises from two ansätze in one sample regime.
- *Technical errors:* The claim that Q-IQP-MMD 'is structure-agnostic on IDQNN' is false. By Def. 16 of 2509.09033 (verified in the full text, p. 42), IDQNN at x=0^n is H^n · CZ_E · RZ(theta) · H^n|0>, i.e. a degree-2 IQP circuit: ZZ angle pi/4 on the grid edges, Z angles -theta_i/2 - (pi/4)·deg(i). check1.py reproduces it with an all-to-all IQP to within 1e-17 on 2x3, 2x5 and 3x3 grids. So under the stated rule, which counts Q-IQP-MMD wins on the IDQNN 2x5 cells, a well-specification effect can be declared a 'quantum-native inductive bias' using 2 of the 4 primary cells.; The Ising/Boltzmann control is contained exactly in the classical baseline families: a pairwise log-linear model, and approximately an RBM. Quantum models therefore cannot win on it, and the specificity condition can never fail.; The pilot's 'sign-resolved estimator reached KL 0.04-0.06' uses oracle signs (idqnn_pilot.py: th_or = where(sin(th)<0, -th_hat, th_hat)), not a data-driven procedure. A data-driven maximum-likelihood sign search over the x=0^n samples does reach the oracle KL (my check), so the claim is right in substance but was not demonstrated by the pilot.; The factor-2 point is confirmed and should stay. Def. 16 uses R_Z(theta)=e^{-i theta Z/2}, while S.4.13 writes Pr[y=1]=(1-cos 2theta)/2 (p. 47), whereas |+>, RZ(theta), X-measurement gives (1-cos theta)/2. S.2.1 is written in beta with ½·arccos, consistent with beta = theta/2. The range of beta used in the experiments was not found: NOT VERIFIED.; The hypothesis clause 'their classical twins match them exactly' is tautological at <= 10 qubits, because the twin is the same model simulated classically. It is not a testable prediction.; Calling a Q-HEA win evidence of a 'quantum-native' bias is wrong at this scale. Every 10-qubit Born machine is a complex MPS with chi <= 32, so (iii) is not specific to quantum models here, contrary to the mechanism field.; In the pilot HEA, each layer ends with a CZ ring right before a computational-basis measurement, so the last entangling layer has no effect. L layers give L-1 effective entangling layers; add a final rotation layer.; The pilot's real-valued MPS ended worse than uniform in one cell (n=10, N=3000, seed 2: 0.64 vs 0.42). The classical MPS baseline is under-optimised as specified.; 'TV 0.50' for the sign flip is a single random instance (idqnn_sign.py, one seed), not a general value. The TV depends on theta.
- *Required fixes:* Reclassify Q-IQP-MMD as structure-aware on IDQNN targets and never count its IDQNN wins. If a misspecified IQP arm is wanted, restrict its ZZ graph to a ring or line that misses the grid's vertical edges, and say so.; Replace the Ising control with controls misspecified for both sides: Boltzmann distributions with 3-body terms; real binary data (bars-and-stripes 2x5, binarized MNIST patches, cardinality n=10/k=5 as in 2303.15626 and 2608.31117); shallow random brickwork circuits outside the Q-HEA family. Match controls on difficulty (lookup-table KL-vs-N curve, KL to uniform), not on Shannon entropy.; Add the data-starved regime: N in {100, 300} at n=10, and report N/2^n. Pre-register that cells with N >= 2^n test only well-specification. Include the computed floors in the design: IQP n=10 lookup table 0.236 / 0.116 / 0.045 at N = 1e3 / 3e3 / 1e4, against d/(2N) = 0.028 / 0.009 / 0.003.; Add an N=infinity arm for every model (fit to the exact target) to measure approximation error. Claim an inductive bias only on estimation error at matched approximation error. This separates a Q-HEA trainability failure (K3) from lack of bias.; Train Q-HEA and the IQP Born machine by exact NLL (affordable at n<=10) as the primary arms. Keep MMD as a secondary, scalability-motivated variant, and report MMD as well as KL, to avoid the objective mismatch shown in 2608.31117.; Add classical baselines with configuration counts at least those of the quantum arms: a complex MPS Born machine trained by two-site sweeps with chi in {2,4,8,16,32}; a fully visible Boltzmann/max-ent model with 2- and 3-body terms and exact partition function; a correlator max-ent surrogate matched to Q-IQP-MMD's moments. Require any Q-HEA win to hold against a complex MPS with equal parameter count.; Drop the IDQNN-specific |0>/|+> input encoding from Q-HEA (train it unconditionally on the x=0^n samples), or give the classical chains the same structural feature. This also removes the ~10x runtime overrun of the conditional variant (0.48 s per step at L=8, batch 256).; Replace oracle signs with a data-driven sign-resolution step: maximum likelihood over sign patterns on the x=0^n samples at n<=10, or local resolution from neighbour correlations. Report sign-blindness as a fixable branch ambiguity, not a headline finding.; Reword the hypothesis, win_teaches and loss_teaches. At n<=10 a win can only show a circuit-parameterisation or geometry bias. Drop the tautological 'classical twins match exactly' clause. Record a loss as 'no generative advantage for HEA/IQP parameterisations at <=10 qubits in regimes X', not 'T3 closed'.; Add an ablation twin of Q-HEA, with no CZ or with the entangler replaced by a Bayesian-network-style circuit, as in 2101.08354, and compare it with the §6 rule, mirroring the repository's entanglement ablation.; Re-budget: estimate about 12 experiments, pre-register which model-cell pairs count, and fix the 'at least 2 of N cells' threshold over Q-HEA cells only.
- *Further prior work:* arXiv:2605.28986 (Full text read. Del Rey, Vallejos, de Melo. A deep energy-based model with exact partition function, trained by maximum likelihood on samples from 10-qubit random MPS and Clifford+T circuits; learnability tracks simulation hardness. Classical learning of circuit distributions at exactly this scale.); arXiv:2010.11983 (Abstract only, resolved by arXiv API. Niu et al., 'Learnability and Complexity of Quantum Samples'. DBM, GAN, LSTM and autoregressive GAN models learn samples from deep random circuits; LSTM does best; learner complexity grows with n.); arXiv:2603.06440 (alphaXiv report only. C.-Y. Liu, Placidi, Brunner, Rinaldi (Quantinuum). Correlation-complexity map. IQP learners on IQP-generated targets (16 qubits), and IQP vs RBM/DCGAN on turbulence with acknowledged untuned baselines. A dataset-level 'IQP-compatibility' diagnostic, relevant to M2.); arXiv:2511.01845 (alphaXiv report only. Herrero-Gonzalez, Coyle et al. Correlator decomposition of QCBMs, dequantisation conditions, MPS and Pauli-propagation surrogates. Inductive-bias experiments at n=6 found no specialised bias for the IQP ansatz. Source of the correlator max-ent surrogate baseline.); arXiv:2101.08354 (Full text pages read (abstract, Secs. II-V). The quantum extension (BBQC) is itself an MPS; numerics on biofam, promoter and SPECT data vs HMMs. The 'quantum-correlation' bias is realised by a classical complex MPS, so the classical baseline must include a complex MPS.); arXiv:2608.14169 (Title and author from the arXiv API only (Roth, 'Classical Limits of Spectral Filtering in Quantum Generative Models'). Classical post-processing matching a coherent quantum generative operation at matched sampling cost. Not read beyond the abstract snippet.)
