## T4. Provable separations on natural distributions of classical data

### I-20261012-01: Periodic neuron learning on Gaussian data with quantum statistical queries

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | external batch A |
| Hypothesis | A quantum statistical query (QSQ) learner learns periodic neurons over Gaussian/logistic inputs with exponentially fewer queries than any classical gradient-based or SQ learner. |
| Mechanism | Lewis, Gilboa & McClean prove periodic neurons are hard for classical gradient methods on non-uniform distributions (Gaussian, logistic) but efficiently learnable in the QSQ model; the quantum learner uses coherent access to the data distribution. |
| Data | Classical inputs (Gaussian/logistic in R^d), labels from a periodic neuron; learner gets quantum query access. |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | d=4 (4-5 qubits, amplitude encoding), N=500 Gaussian points, target sin(a.x+b); QSQ learner estimating Fourier coefficients by Hadamard tests; baselines: MLP with sinusoidal activations, RBF kernel ridge regression, random Fourier features; metric: queries/gradient evaluations to reach MSE<=0.05; success: quantum <=1e3 queries, classical >=1e5 or fail. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | Separation is asymptotic; at d=4 constants may erase it. |
| References cited | 2503.20879, 2011.01938, 2403.07059 |
| Reference checks | 2503.20879: **OK** (Full text v1 and partial v2 read. QSQ learning of periodic neurons over near-flat Gaussian/generalized-Gaussian/logistic product distributions; hardness for gradient methods (and correlational SQ for wide Gaussians in v2) is in the paper. The card's 'any classical gradient-based OR SQ learner' overreaches: general-SQ hardness is not proven (the paper says refs [82,83] do not apply to its parameter regime).); 2011.01938: **OK** (Exists (power of data / geometric difference). Context only; says nothing about periodic neurons.); 2403.07059: **OK** (Exists (Bowles, Ahmed, Schuld benchmark). Context only.) |
| Technical problems | 'd=4 (4-5 qubits, amplitude encoding)' cannot hold the discretized quantum example state sum_x sqrt(D(x))\|x>\|h(x)>. It needs d*log2(2*R*M1) input qubits plus a label register; at <= 10 qubits only d = 1-2 with 3-4 qubits per coordinate fit.; 'QSQ learner estimating Fourier coefficients by Hadamard tests' is not the LGM algorithm. LGM uses QFT-based Fourier sampling on one coordinate register, Hallgren-style continued fractions, and a shift-correlation verification observable.; The separation is vacuous at d=4: the gradient bound p/eps' with eps' ~ sqrt(exp(-c2 d)+...) is O(1), and w* is drawn from a 0.51-packing net of the positive-orthant cap that has only a handful of points at d=4, so ERM over the net needs O(log\|C\|) samples.; The success threshold 'quantum <= 1e3 queries, classical >= 1e5 or fail' is not derived from anything. It also compares QSQ counts (each costing O(1/tau^2) state copies, tau ~ 1/M2^2) with gradient evaluations.; The claim 'than any SQ learner' is not proven: v2 proves hardness only against gradient and correlational-SQ algorithms (Gaussian, sigma >= Omega~(d^2), tolerance O(1/d^4)).; 'N=500 Gaussian points' is a misconception: the QSQ learner does not use classical samples. If its state is loaded (QRAM-style) from N random examples with distinct other coordinates, the Fourier-sampling QSQ returns a flat distribution for d >= 2 (checked numerically in the scratchpad). The quantum learner therefore needs a coherent oracle for the unknown target, strictly more access than the classical side gets.; 'Gaussian' in LGM means nearly flat over the truncated box (sigma = Omega(R), e.g. Sigma = 4 pi R^2 I), not N(0, I). |
| Kill-criteria check | K6, K7, K4 (compares QSQ counts with gradient evaluations and omits the strongest classical learners: ERM over the finite concept class, LLL, membership queries) |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2503.20879 (The theorem itself (QSQ upper bound; gradient and correlational-SQ lower bounds).); arXiv:1609.01037 (Shamir: gradient-based hardness for periodic targets under nice distributions.); arXiv:2106.10744 (LLL learns noiseless cosine neurons on N(0,I) with d+1 samples, so SQ/gradient hardness is not hardness for all classical algorithms.) |
| Status | **REJECTED** |
| Decision note | There is nothing to measure at d=4. The gradient lower bound is vacuous there, and the concept class (a 0.51-packing net) is finite and tiny, so ERM or LLL learns it from tens of samples. The asymptotic theorem is already proven in 2503.20879, so a d=4 simulation adds nothing testable (K6, K7). Its subject survives only inside the access-matched audit of I-20261012-39. |

### I-20261012-13: Learning hidden-shift / discrete-log concept classes with the quantum Fourier transform

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | external batch B |
| Hypothesis | A shallow quantum learner outperforms all polynomial-size classical kernel methods on concept classes defined by discrete logarithms or hidden shifts. |
| Mechanism | QFT structure bypasses classical cryptographic/parity lower bounds. |
| Data | Classical bitstrings from a hidden-shift distribution. |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | 8-qubit hidden-shift circuit vs tuned kernel ridge regression and random forests; metric: generalization error vs sample size; success: zero error with exponentially fewer samples. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | Real data rarely has algebraic hidden-shift structure. |
| References cited | 2503.20879, 2010.02174 |
| Reference checks | 2503.20879: **CLAIM NOT IN PAPER** (The paper is about periodic neurons and Hallgren-type period finding over non-uniform distributions. It contains no hidden-shift or discrete-log concept class.); 2010.02174: **CLAIM NOT IN PAPER** (The DLP concept class with a separation against ALL efficient classical learners is in the paper (abstract read; FIELD_MAP [F]). But its learner is an SVM whose kernel needs a fault-tolerant quantum computer running Shor-type circuits, not a 'shallow quantum learner', and the paper has no hidden-shift class.) |
| Technical problems | Lookup/brute force: with n = 8 there are at most 256 group elements or shifts, so a classical exhaustive learner is exact with O(n) samples. 'Exponentially fewer samples' is meaningless at this size.; 'Shallow' contradicts the cited mechanism: the Liu-Arunachalam-Temme feature map requires discrete-log (Shor) circuits, i.e. fault tolerance.; Hidden shift is not a PAC-from-random-examples separation. Fourier sampling uniform quantum examples of a shifted bent function gives a uniformly random y (\|f_hat\|^2 is flat), so the shift is found only by using the dual bent function's phases. For Maiorana-McFarland f(u,v) = u.pi(v) xor h(v) with efficiently invertible pi (which the quantum algorithm also needs, through the dual), O(n) classical membership queries recover the shift by GF(2) linear algebra.; The baselines (tuned kernel ridge, random forests) are generic learners, not the strongest classical algorithm for the task (classical discrete log or linear algebra at 8 bits).; These concept classes are cryptographic, not 'natural distributions of classical data' (the card's own risk statement), so the idea is off-theme. |
| Kill-criteria check | K6, K7, K4 |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2010.02174 (Proven DLP learning separation; needs fault-tolerant Shor-type kernel estimation.); arXiv:2610.04543 (10-14-bit DLP quantum-kernel demo against a generic classical monitor at chance. At those sizes brute-force discrete log is trivial, the same flaw.); arXiv:2306.16028 (Known classical-data separations rest on hardness of evaluating the target, not identifying it.) |
| Status | **REJECTED** |
| Decision note | At 8 qubits a lookup table solves the discrete log (p ~ 2^8), and brute force over 256 shifts solves the hidden shift, so 'zero error with exponentially fewer samples' cannot be observed (K6). The DLP separation is already proven by Liu et al. with a fault-tolerant learner, and a small-scale DLP-kernel demo with exactly this flaw already exists (2610.04543) (K7). The baselines (kernel ridge, random forests) are not the right classical competitors (K4). |

### I-20261012-21: Noise-robust quantum learning of periodic neurons

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | external batch C |
| Hypothesis | A QSQ learner keeps lower query complexity than tuned MLP, RBF-SVM and Fourier-feature learners on periodic-neuron classification over Gaussian/logistic distributions under bounded label noise and finite precision. |
| Mechanism | Quantum statistical-query advantage for periodic functions; classical lower bounds only cover SQ and gradient learners. |
| Data | Classical Gaussian/logistic inputs, labels sign(cos(w.x+b)) with label noise. |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | (1) Small discretized instances d<=8 vs tuned MLP, RBF-SVM, Fourier regression, GBT; (2) decisive: prove/refute that the QSQ learner stays polynomial-query while classical SQ learners need exponentially many queries, for a given noise rate and finite precision. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | Separation may depend on the query model; noise may break it. |
| References cited | 2503.20879, 2306.16028 |
| Reference checks | 2503.20879: **OK** (QSQ advantage for periodic neurons is in the paper. 'Classical lower bounds only cover SQ and gradient learners' is roughly right but actually narrower: only gradient and correlational SQ (v2), not general SQ. The paper does not treat label noise beyond QSQ tolerance.); 2306.16028: **OK** (Exists (Gyurik & Dunjko); general discussion of how definitions and access change separations. Context only.) |
| Technical problems | Classical SQ lower bounds under label noise follow from noiseless ones, because an SQ to the noisy distribution is a fixed linear combination of SQs to the noiseless one. 'Classical SQ needs exponentially many queries under noise' adds nothing. Even the noiseless general-SQ bound is unproven in LGM's regime (only correlational SQ).; 'Finite precision' is already the QSQ tolerance tau (~1/poly in LGM), so it is not a new axis.; Generic robustness to noisy quantum examples is already known: a QSQ learner implies an eta-noisy quantum PAC learner for tau > sqrt(eta) (2002.08240). Real-valued label noise of size beta >~ 1/M2 changes LGM's level sets and can break pseudoperiodicity, so robustness at that size is not automatic.; The all-algorithm classical hardness at beta = 1/poly (2106.10744) is proven for x ~ N(0,I) and gamma >= 2 sqrt(d). LGM's discretized, box-truncated, near-flat Gaussian is outside that theorem (LGM say so explicitly).; Labels 'sign(cos(w.x+b))' change the problem: LGM's class is real-valued sum_j beta_j cos(2 pi j x.w) under squared loss.; The baselines in part (1) omit membership-query learners, which have the classical counterpart of the oracle that quantum examples presuppose. |
| Kill-criteria check | K7 (generic noise robustness of QSQ learners is already in 2002.08240; tolerance-based QSQ-vs-SQ separations exist in 2602.02008), K6 for part (1): d <= 8 instances cannot exhibit an asymptotic separation |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2602.02008 (Tolerance-based QSQ-vs-SQ separation where quantum learners survive noise levels that defeat classical SQ (symmetric function classes).); arXiv:1702.08255 (LWE is easy with quantum samples. A noisy-periodic-neuron (CLWE) analogue would be the same access-model pattern.); arXiv:2002.08240 (QSQ implies noise-robust quantum PAC learning (tau > sqrt(eta)).); arXiv:2106.10744 (Noise regime where classical random-example learning is hard for all poly-time algorithms (N(0,I), gamma >= 2 sqrt d).) |
| Status | **MERGED into I-20261012-39** |
| Decision note | Its only non-trivial content is the noise axis of I-39: does the quantum learner survive 1/poly label noise, where classical hardness extends to all poly-time algorithms (Song-Zadik-Bruna)? The classical-SQ half of its decisive theorem is automatic. Any resulting separation would still be against random-example/SQ access only, because membership queries emulate the QSQ learner for any fixed or fresh-noise labelling. |

### I-20261012-29: Stress-testing published natural-distribution separations

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | external batch D |
| Hypothesis | The quantum learner of a recent natural-distribution separation paper beats carefully tuned classical models on that paper's own task and distribution. |
| Mechanism | Targets a published structured distribution rather than a generic feature map. |
| Data | Classical samples from the exact distribution of arXiv:2503.20879 or arXiv:2405.02027, smallest nontrivial instances (<=10 qubits). |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | Implement the paper's learner and instance generator unchanged; vs nested-tuned RBF-SVM, GBT, RF, MLP; held-out accuracy and sample efficiency over seeds; success: robust quantum win over every baseline. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | Advantage may need larger instances or a narrow classical-model class. |
| References cited | 2503.20879, 2405.02027 |
| Reference checks | 2503.20879: **OK** (Natural-distribution QSQ separation; full text read.); 2405.02027: **OK** (Exists (Molteni, Gyurik, Dunjko): learning quantum observables from measured-out data, with hardness from BQP processes not being simulable by poly-size classical circuits. It is quantum-physics data, not a natural distribution of classical data, and its hardness cannot appear at <= 10 qubits.) |
| Technical problems | 'Implement unchanged' is impossible within the limits. LGM's parameters (M1 = max(70 pi d D^3 R_w, R_w^2/eps1), R~ = Omega~(tau M1^2 d^4/R_w^2, ...)) give coordinate registers far beyond 10 qubits for any d >= 1. Only reduced-constant instances with d <= 2 fit, and there no separation exists.; The success rule 'robust quantum win over every baseline' cannot be met fairly. At feasible sizes, ERM over the 0.51-packing net or exhaustive search learns w* from O(d) samples. Quantum examples presuppose a coherent oracle for the target, so the fair classical competitor has membership queries.; The 2405.02027 task needs BQP-hard observables to be classically intractable. At <= 10 qubits they are exactly simulable, so no separation can appear. |
| Kill-criteria check | K6 (as posed), K4 (as posed: only random-example baselines against a learner with coherent oracle access) |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2503.20879 (The separation to be stress-tested.); arXiv:2403.07059 (Benchmarking discipline: tuned baselines and ablations.); arXiv:2405.02027 (Second candidate separation, quantum-observable data; not testable at this scale.) |
| Status | **MERGED into I-20261012-39** |
| Decision note | Running LGM's learner 'unchanged' at <= 10 qubits against random-example baselines has a predetermined and uninformative outcome. At d <= 2 with a finite concept class, classical ERM wins. A quantum 'win' would only reflect the extra oracle access. The idea survives only as the implementation backbone of I-39's access-matched audit. The 2405.02027 branch is K6, since BQP-hardness is vacuous at <= 10 simulated qubits. |

### I-20261012-39: Classical lattice (LLL) baseline for periodic-neuron separations

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | Claude |
| Hypothesis | For noiseless periodic neurons on Gaussian inputs at d<=8, a classical lattice-basis-reduction learner matches the quantum learner's sample efficiency, so the separation of arXiv:2503.20879 holds only against SQ/gradient learners; the meaningful regime is with label noise. |
| Mechanism | Lattice methods learn noiseless cosine neurons; with enough noise the problem is cryptographically hard for all polynomial-time classical algorithms. |
| Data | Classical. |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | d=2..8, N up to 1e4; simulated quantum learner vs gradient-trained MLPs, random Fourier features and an LLL learner; error vs samples/queries across noise levels; success: identify a noise regime where the quantum learner beats LLL and gradient methods. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | Quantum example access requires coherent data loading (K2-like); lattice papers' IDs to verify. |
| References cited | 2503.20879, Song-Zadik-Bruna and Zadik-Song-Wein-Bruna lattice papers: IDs to verify |
| Reference checks | 2503.20879: **OK** (Hardness only against gradient methods (and correlational SQ in v2), as the card says; full text read.); Song-Zadik-Bruna lattice paper (ID to verify): **OK** (Verified as arXiv:2106.10744 (Song, Zadik, Bruna), 'On the Cryptographic Hardness of Learning Single Periodic Neurons'. Theorem 3.6: LLL with d+1 samples for noise <= exp(-(d log d)^3), 1 <= gamma <= d^Q, x ~ N(0,I). Corollary 3.4: all-poly-time hardness at beta = 1/poly, gamma >= 2 sqrt(d), under worst-case lattice assumptions. Partial text read.); Zadik-Song-Wein-Bruna lattice paper (ID to verify): **CLAIM NOT IN PAPER** (Verified as arXiv:2112.03898 (Zadik, Song, Wein, Bruna), 'Lattice-Based Methods Surpass Sum-of-Squares in Clustering'. It applies LLL to Gaussian-mixture clustering (planted hypercube vector), not to cosine neurons. It is only a precedent for LLL beating SoS and low-degree methods. Abstract read.) |
| Technical problems | 'LLL matches the quantum learner' is unproven for LGM's actual task and fails asymptotically. SZB's LLL needs noise <= exp(-(d log d)^3), continuous Gaussian inputs (anticoncentration of the Cramer-rule coefficients) and a single cosine. LGM's discretized task has labels rounded to 1/M2 with M2 ~ 9dD^2 (noise ~ 1/poly), grid-valued inputs, and D >= 1 unknown harmonics.; At d <= 8 any comparison is uninformative: ERM over the finite 0.51-packing net learns w* from O(d) samples, and the gradient-hardness bounds are O(1).; 'The meaningful regime is with label noise' holds only for random-example classical learners (CLWE hardness for N(0,I), gamma >= 2 sqrt d). It does not change the access-matched comparison, because the membership-query emulation of QSQs is exact for any labelling function, fixed or with fresh noise.; The success rule 'identify a noise regime where the quantum learner beats LLL and gradient methods' is satisfied trivially by the access asymmetry (coherent oracle versus random examples) and must be replaced by an access-matched rule.; 'Quantum example access requires coherent data loading' understates the problem. Loading the N classical examples does not even work: for d >= 2, with distinct other coordinates, the reduced state of each coordinate register is diagonal and Fourier sampling returns uniform outcomes (checked numerically). |
| Kill-criteria check | K7 risk: the LLL baseline itself is already in 2106.10744 (d+1 samples, exponentially small noise). Only the access-matched part is new; no prior statement of it was found in 6 searches, but folklore cannot be excluded., K6: avoided only by stating the decisive claim as a theorem and checking it exactly at <= 10 qubits; a small-d empirical 'separation' would be meaningless, K4: avoided by adding the access-matched membership-query learner and ERM over the finite class to tuned random-example baselines |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2106.10744 (LLL learner for noiseless cosine neurons and all-algorithm hardness under 1/poly noise.); arXiv:2503.20879 (The QSQ separation under audit.); arXiv:1702.08255 (Same access pattern for LWE: hard with classical samples, easy with quantum samples.); arXiv:2002.08240 (QSQ replaces classical membership queries in Goldreich-Levin, the converse direction of the emulation proposed here.); arXiv:2112.09104 (Another LLL algorithm defeating SQ-type lower bounds (NGCA).); arXiv:2602.01054 (Survey framing quantum learning speedups as effects of the labeling-oracle model.) |
| Status | **SHORTLISTED (weakened in adversarial review: apply the required fixes before building)** |
| Decision note | Shortlisted as the base of the theme's best version, but sharpened. The strongest classical competitor is not LLL: its guarantee needs exponentially small noise, continuous Gaussian inputs and a pure cosine, and LGM's 1/M2 label rounding, grid inputs and D harmonics break all three. It is a classical learner with membership queries, the classical form of the oracle access the quantum example state already requires. |
| Decisive test | Theorem plus exact check. Claim: every QSQ of LGM's algorithm can be answered classically. (a) The coordinate-local Fourier-sampling observable (eqs. D.22, E.39, E.70) equals E_{x_-j}[sum_y <phi_y\|O_j\|phi_y>], computed from one fiber of q membership queries plus an FFT. (b) The shift-verification observable (eqs. D.48-D.52) equals a pairwise shift correlation from 2 membership queries per sample. So each QSQ is answered to tolerance tau with O(q log(1/delta)/tau^2) membership queries. Check: run LGM's pipeline with exact statevector QSQs and with MQ-emulated QSQs (d in {1,2}, <= 10 qubits, 5 seeds), plus a direct multi-scale MQ learner at d up to 64. Decision: LGM's separation is classed as an access-model separation if the emulated answers lie within tau of the exact QSQ values on >= 99% of queries, and the MQ pipeline recovers w-hat on at least as many seeds as the exact pipeline at every size. Prototype in the scratchpad: the identity holds to 1e-12 on a 9-qubit instance. |

### T4 best version: Access-matched audit of the periodic-neuron QSQ separation (arXiv:2503.20879): do classical membership queries dequantize it?

*Kind:* killing experiment. *Built from:* I-20261012-39, I-20261012-29, I-20261012-21, I-20261012-01.

- **Hypothesis:** The exponential quantum advantage of Lewis, Gilboa and McClean for learning periodic neurons over near-flat Gaussian product distributions separates quantum-example (QSQ) access from classical random-example, gradient or correlational-SQ access. It does not separate a quantum learner from a classical learner given the same oracle. Three parts: (a) Every QSQ the quantum learner makes can be answered to tolerance tau by O(q log(1/delta)/tau^2) classical membership queries (MQ) to the same target, where q is the number of grid points per coordinate. So a classical MQ learner matches it up to a factor poly(d, D, R_w, 1/eps). (b) A direct multi-scale classical MQ learner also matches its polylog(R_w) scaling. (c) For d >= 2, the quantum learner gets no period information if its example states are built by loading the classical learner's random examples.
- **Mechanism:** LGM's quantum learner uses two kinds of QSQ on |h> = sum_x sqrt(D(x)) |x>|h(x)>, with D a product distribution. (1) Fourier sampling on ONE coordinate register: O_j = QFT_j^dag diag(l/M) QFT_j, identity on the other coordinates and on the label (eqs. D.22, E.39, E.70). Tracing out the other coordinates and the label gives <h|O_j|h> = E_{x_-j ~ D_-j}[ sum_y <phi_y|O_j|phi_y> ], where phi_y = sum over {x_j : h(x_j, x_-j) = y} of sqrt(D_j(x_j)) |x_j>. The bracket lies in [0,1] and is computable from the q labels of one axis-parallel fiber (q membership queries) plus one FFT per level set. Hoeffding's inequality then gives tolerance tau with O(log(1/delta)/tau^2) fibers. (2) Verification: O_{k,m} = A_m S_{k,-T}, whose expectation is the shift correlation mean_x h(x) h(x + T e_k)/M2^2 (eq. D.52). It needs 2 membership queries per Monte Carlo sample, with an importance weight sqrt(D(x+T e_k)/D(x)) for flat non-uniform D. Both identities hold for ANY labelling, including label noise, so noise cannot create a separation against MQ access. If instead the state is loaded from N random examples whose other coordinates are all distinct, the reduced state of coordinate j is diagonal and Fourier sampling is uniform: the quantum learner needs the coherent oracle. Anyone holding that oracle (in LGM's own proposed use case, a known circuit or trained network) can answer classical membership queries. The classical learners in LGM's lower bounds (gradient, correlational SQ) and LLL (random examples) never get this access, so the separation is an access-model separation. Scratchpad prototype (round01/t4_sim): identities (1) and (2) match exact statevector values to 1e-12 on a 9-qubit instance (d=2, 3 qubits per coordinate), and (c) gives exactly uniform outcomes.
- **Data:** Synthetic: LGM's concept class. - w* in the positive orthant with w_j >= R_w/d^2, sampled from a greedy 0.51-packing net (exactly LGM's class for d <= 8; random admissible w beyond). - D in {1, 2, 3} harmonics with ||beta||_1 = 1. - Product input distributions: uniform on the grid, and discrete Gaussian with sigma = Omega(R) (LGM's flatness condition). - Optional label noise beta_noise in {0, 1/(4 M2), 1/M2}, either as a fixed function of x or fresh per query. - Sizes: d in {1, 2} for the statevector comparisons; d in {2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64} and R_w in {sqrt(d), d, 2^10} for the classical-only runs. - Seeds 0-4. Test loss is estimated on 10^4 fresh points from D.
- **Quantum model:** LGM's QSQ learner with reduced (practical) constants. - Discretized, truncated example state: inputs on a grid of spacing 1/M1 in [-R, R)^d; labels rounded down to multiples of 1/M2. - Exact statevector QSQs (numpy/PennyLane) for d=1 (6-7 coordinate qubits plus 3 label qubits) and d=2 (3-4 qubits per coordinate plus 2-3 label qubits), at most 10 qubits. - Step 1: Hallgren-style period recovery from two Fourier samples via continued fractions, with shift-correlation verification of candidate periods. - Step 2: gradient-descent fit of beta given w-hat. - Run with tau-perturbed QSQ answers (adversarial within tau, and finite-shot, R = O(1/tau^2) copies per query).
- **Classical baselines:** (i) Access-matched: the same LGM pipeline with every QSQ replaced by the Monte Carlo MQ emulator above (same tau and delta). (ii) Direct multi-scale MQ learner: queries g along each axis at geometrically spaced points, Prony/ESPRIT on the D harmonics, scale doubling as in robust phase estimation; O(dD log(R_w/eps)) queries expected. (iii) Random-example learners, tuned by grid search on the same 3 folds, selected by validation squared loss: - MLP with ReLU and with sin activations: hidden {16, 64, 256}, alpha {1e-4, 1e-2, 1}, learning rate {1e-3, 1e-2}; - random Fourier features + ridge: bandwidth x {256, 2048} features x lambda; - RBF kernel ridge: gamma x lambda; - HistGradientBoosting with the DESIGN.md section 4 grid; - ERM over the 0.51-packing net for d <= 8; - optionally, Song-Zadik-Bruna's LLL (Algorithm 2 of 2106.10744) for D=1, on exact labels and on 1/M2-rounded labels. Grid-edge rule as in DESIGN.md section 4, applied to both families.
- **Access model:** Quantum learner: copies of |h> (each copy = 1 coherent call to U_h: |x>|0> -> |x>|h(x)>, plus Grover-Rudolph preparation of sqrt(D)). Single-copy measurements only (QSQ), no quantum memory across copies; tolerance tau implemented with O(1/tau^2) copies per query. It may choose (M1, M2, R) per query, as in LGM's Definition 1. Classical learner, LGM's comparison: approximate gradients of the population loss / correlational SQs / random examples (x, h(x)) with x ~ D. Classical learner, access-matched: membership queries h(x) at chosen grid points, one classical call to the same oracle per query; any discretization is computable from MQs to g. Oracle calls are counted on both sides: copies of the state versus MQ calls versus random examples. Statement of fairness: when quantum examples are manufactured from an oracle, restricting the classical side to random examples is an arbitrary handicap, not the M8 measure-then-process restriction. If such states came from nature with no queryable oracle, the remaining separation would be computational-basis versus Fourier-basis single-copy measurement, not a quantum-memory separation, and no physical source of such states for classical data is known.
- **Metric:** (1) |emulated - exact| QSQ value per query. (2) Success rate of ||w-hat - w*||_inf <= eps1. (3) Test squared loss L(theta-hat) relative to Var(g), with a target of eps = 0.01 Var(g). (4) Oracle calls needed to reach the target: state copies (quantum), MQ calls (classical access-matched), labelled examples (random-example learners). (5) Wall-clock time per seed.
- **Success rule:** Pre-registered before any code is run. The dequantization claim C is CONFIRMED if all three hold: (i) At every tested instance (d in {1,2}, 5 seeds, every query the pipeline issues), the exact fiber formula reproduces the statevector QSQ value to < 1e-9, and the Monte Carlo MQ emulator lies within tau on >= 99% of queries. (ii) The MQ-emulated pipeline recovers w-hat (||w-hat - w*||_inf <= eps1) on at least as many seeds as the exact-QSQ pipeline, at every size and noise level. (iii) The direct multi-scale MQ learner reaches L <= 0.01 Var(g) on >= 4 of 5 seeds at every d <= 64 and R_w <= 2^10. Its MQ count must fit a log-log slope <= 2 in d and grow at most polylogarithmically in R_w (fitted exponent <= 3 in log R_w). Part (a) alone is confirmed by (i) and (ii). Part (b) needs (iii). C is REFUTED if: - any QSQ used by LGM (including the Appendix E verification observables) is found to be neither coordinate-local nor a single-coordinate shift times a label operator; or - (ii) fails on >= 2 of 5 seeds at some size. Secondary, report only: apply the DESIGN.md section 6 paired rule (5 seeds, >= 4/5 and mean paired difference > sd) between the MQ learner and each tuned random-example learner at matched oracle budgets N in {1e2, 1e3, 1e4, 1e5}. Report the smallest d at which each random-example learner fails, as a replication of the known gradient-hardness trend (1609.01037; LGM Theorem 1).
- **Milestone:** M1 (the natural-distribution entry for 2503.20879), with an access-model caveat cross-referenced to M8 (cf. 2609.38403).
- **Kill check:** Before writing code: re-read LGM's Appendix E verification step (Theorem 12 and its observables) and list every observable the algorithm queries. If each is coordinate-local or a single-coordinate shift times a label-register operator, proceed. If any is non-local and non-product, part (a) is false as stated and the audit must target that query. Also re-run the two scratch prototypes inside the repository's test suite.
- **Cost estimate:** Code: - New module src/qmlrc/periodic_neuron.py (~300 lines): instances and packing net; discretization; statevector QSQs; MQ emulator; continued-fraction period recovery and verification; direct MQ learner. - Optional src/qmlrc/lattice.py (~100 lines): pure-Python integer LLL for D=1 and d <= 8. - Experiment wiring in qmlrc.experiments (~60 lines) and tests (~120 lines). - Total ~480-580 lines. CPU (one laptop core): - Statevector identity checks: < 1 min. - Exact and emulated pipelines at <= 10 qubits, 5 seeds: ~2 min. - Direct MQ learner up to d=64: < 1 min. - Tuned random-example baselines (MLP capped at N = 1e4): ~6-8 min. - Split into two experiments, each under 10 min. Theory: a 2-3 page proof of parts (a) and (c) for DESIGN.md. Part (b) is easy for D=1; for D > 1 it may remain a stated conjecture with numerical support. The two scratch prototypes (~90 lines) already exist under round01/t4_sim.
- **Win teaches:** If C is confirmed (the expected outcome): the M1 entry for 2503.20879 must say the advantage is over random-example, gradient and correlational-SQ classical learners only. With the oracle that the quantum example state presupposes, a classical learner matches it up to polynomial factors (or polylog factors, if (b) holds). 'Natural input distributions' therefore do not yet give a classical-data QML advantage. T4 effort should move to separations in which the quantum learner also receives only classical samples (open per 2608.11648), or to M8 settings where quantum states come from physical processes.
- **Loss teaches:** If C is refuted (some LGM query is not MQ-emulable, or emulation fails where exact QSQs succeed): the periodic-neuron advantage survives an access-matched classical baseline. That would be the strongest natural-distribution separation known and a substantive upgrade of M1. It would justify isolating which non-local quantum statistic does the work and checking its robustness to shot noise and to finite tau.
- **Closest prior work:** arXiv:2503.20879 (The separation under audit: QSQ upper bound; gradient and correlational-SQ lower bounds only.); arXiv:2106.10744 (LLL beats SQ/gradient bounds for noiseless cosine neurons; all-algorithm hardness with 1/poly noise for random examples.); arXiv:1702.08255 (Same access pattern for LWE: hard with classical samples, easy with quantum samples.); arXiv:2002.08240 (QSQ model; QSQ replaces classical MQ in Goldreich-Levin (converse direction); noise robustness tau > sqrt(eta).); arXiv:2602.01054 (Survey: quantum learning speedups as consequences of the labeling-oracle model.); arXiv:2609.38403 (Access model (forward-only versus inverse) decides whether quantum PAC data gives any advantage.); arXiv:2608.11648 (Quantum versus classical examples for quantum learners is still open (only an oracle-relative separation).)

**Adversarial review: WEAKENED.** I tried four angles and failed to break the core claim, but the proposal survives only in a weakened form. **Core claim holds.** Every QSQ that Lewis-Gilboa-McClean (LGM, 2503.20879) use can be emulated by classical membership queries (MQ). I checked all of LGM's observables: each acts on one coordinate, or is a single-coordinate shift times a label operator. The scratch identities hold to about 1e-12. The fairness argument is also sound: building the example state needs a coherent oracle, and whoever has that oracle can answer MQs. **(a) Already done.** No paper does this for LGM, so K7 does not kill it. Novelty is low: 2610.02636 (Oct 2026) already publishes the same access-matching method for Fourier sampling. LGM v2 itself concedes that example states need c* to be known. **(b) Classical can match.** The hypothesis wrongly says LGM separates QSQ from classical random-example access. LGM proves only gradient and correlational-SQ hardness. For exact labels, the LLL algorithm of 2106.10744 (Thm 3.6) already learns D=1 cosine neurons from d+1 random examples in poly time. With exact real-valued MQ, part (b) is trivial (d queries for D=1). **(c) Access model.** The wording needs correcting: realizability, not oracle equality. The noise claim fails for coherent label noise, and part (c) holds only for coordinate-local observables. There may also be a mismatch between LGM's truncated quantum distribution and the untruncated Gaussian of its classical bounds; I have not verified this. **(d) Feasibility and decisiveness.** - LGM's QSQ returns a mean, not a Fourier sample: their eqs. D141-D142 are wrong, and the observable's value is about 0.424 for every period tested. The pipeline must use projector QSQs with CDF bisection. - The test instances are degenerate. At d=1, w is known in advance; the packing net has 2 points at d=2 and about 99 at d=8. The d=2 registers are sub-Nyquist. Faithful LGM parameters need about 18 qubits per coordinate even at d=1. - The pass criteria follow from the proof plus Hoeffding, so the run cannot decide anything the proof has not. - The R_w grid cannot tell polylog from small-power polynomial growth. - The cost of the tuned baselines is underestimated by at least 100x (measured HistGradientBoosting fits: 0.3-1.4 s each). **Verdict and fixes.** Keep it as a short theorem plus verification code, labelled 'application of a known access-matching principle'. The fixes: - correct the hypothesis; - repair the sampling step; - use non-degenerate instances; - compare against the finite-shot quantum pipeline; - sweep R_w over a wide range; - add LLL on exact and rounded labels; - cut the baselines to a measured budget. Scratch files written: /tmp/claude-0/-home-user-quantum-ml-reality-check/3242e8f8-b193-546c-b381-3fe888e7979d/scratchpad/round01/t4_review/{qsq_mean.py, netsize.py, hgb_time.py}. arxiv_search.py calls used: 1 of 6. No repository file was changed.

- *Already done:* I searched alphaXiv (3 discovery searches; PDF reads of 2503.20879 v2, 2610.02636, 2602.01054 and 2106.10744) and made 1 arxiv_search.py call ('periodic/cosine neuron'). No paper states the LGM-specific result: that every QSQ in 2503.20879 can be emulated by classical membership queries, using a fiber identity for coordinate-local observables and pairwise queries for the shift-verification observable. The LGM-specific theorem therefore appears unpublished, so K7 does not kill it. Its novelty is low, for three reasons. (1) The method itself is published. 2610.02636 (Friedewald, Shiri Sichani, Shyu, Oct 2026, NeurIPS workshop; full text read) runs exactly this kind of 'access-matched audit' for quantum Fourier sampling of delay-PUF models. Its stated principle is that "constructing the phase oracle logically implies classical membership access", with Kushilevitz-Mansour as the matched classical comparator, and it finds no end-to-end advantage. It does not discuss LGM or periodic neurons, so the proposal applies a published principle to a new paper. (2) LGM v2 already concedes the oracle problem. Its Discussion says example states are easy to make only when c* is known, and its main text notes that "only one type of query made by our quantum algorithm is not a correlational QSQ". It claims hardness only against gradient methods and, for Gaussians, correlational SQ (v2 Thms 1 and 2/6). (3) The weaker claim that LGM's separation is not against all classical random-example learners is already true in the noiseless case. 2106.10744 (Song, Zadik, Bruna; Secs 1-3 and App D read) gives an LLL algorithm that learns cos(2*pi*gamma<w,x>) from d+1 noiseless Gaussian examples in poly(d) time for 1 <= gamma <= d^Q and noise beta <= exp(-(d log d)^3). The authors say explicitly that it bypasses the SQ and gradient lower bounds. Learning becomes cryptographically hard at beta = 1/poly with gamma >= 2*sqrt(d). The QEX-versus-MQ relationship is classical background: Bshouty-Jackson, cited in both 2610.02636 and 2602.01054, but not on arXiv and NOT VERIFIED here; 2602.01054 Thm 3.1 shows QEX cannot simulate QMEM. The result should be labelled 'application of a known access-matching principle to LGM', not a new separation result.
- *Classical can match:* The proposal is itself a 'classical can match' argument, and I could not break its core. I reread LGM's observables in v1 and v2: the Fourier observable (D19/D22/E39/E70) acts on one coordinate only, and the verification observable is O_{k,m} = A_m S_{k,-T} (D48-D52, cyclic shift of one coordinate times a label-register operator). Both are emulable by MQ as claimed. The scratch prototype's identities hold to about 1e-12, and the QRAM-loaded state gives exactly uniform Fourier outcomes. What the proposal underuses or misses: (i) LLL with random examples (2106.10744, Thm 3.6) already matches LGM for exact labels with D=1 and gamma = sigma*R_w <= poly(d). It needs no membership queries. The hypothesis wrongly lists 'classical random-example access' as something LGM separates from. Caveats: the adaptation of LLL to LGM's discretized, truncated, near-flat Gaussian is unproven, and with labels rounded to 1/M2 (as in the quantum state) the status is open (CLWE-like). (ii) With exact real-valued MQ, part (b) is trivial. For D=1, w_j = arccos(g(t e_j))/(2*pi*t) for any t < 1/(2 R_w): d queries in total, since the norm is known. For D>1, Prony on 2D axis samples suffices. The only non-trivial MQ version is with labels rounded to 1/M2 and tau-bounded query noise, and the proposal should say so. (iii) At the sizes the proposal tests, everything classical works trivially. LGM's class (B.1 plus a 0.51 angular packing net) has a single admissible w at d=1 (w = R_w, known in advance). My greedy net sizes are 2 at d=2, 5 at d=3, 10 at d=4 and about 99 at d=8. ERM over the net, and any random-example learner, will succeed, so small-d success says nothing about hardness.
- *Access model issue:* The central fairness argument is correct and physically meaningful. A quantum example state of classical data is realizable only by running a coherent oracle for h (in LGM's own proposed use case, a known circuit or trained network). Whoever holds that oracle can answer classical MQs, so restricting the classical side to gradients or correlational SQ is a handicap, not the M8 'measure-then-process' restriction. 2610.02636 makes the same argument for PUF oracles. Corrections needed: (1) Do not call it 'the same oracle'. QEX and MQ are different access types, and QEX cannot simulate (Q)MEM (2602.01054 Thm 3.1). The claim is about realizability: preparing |h> from a circuit implies MQ access. It is not oracle equality. (2) 'Noise cannot create a separation against MQ' holds for deterministic labelings and for independent classical noise mirrored on the MQ side. It does not hold for coherent (QREX-type) label superpositions. There the reduced state contains cross terms sqrt(p(y|x) p(y|x')), which single MQ answers do not give. Restrict the claim, or treat it separately. (3) Part (c), 'no period information from loaded examples', holds only for coordinate-local observables, i.e. LGM's algorithm. A general learner holding the loaded state has exactly the information in the N samples. That information suffices information-theoretically (ERM), and computationally for exact labels (LLL). (4) A further mismatch, my inference and to be verified: the quantum state lives on the truncated, renormalized box [-R,R)^d with s_j >= 2R*sqrt(pi) (LGM E8), so per-coordinate box mass is erf(1/(2*sqrt(pi))) ≈ 0.31 and total mass is about 0.31^d. LGM's classical lower bounds are stated for the untruncated Gaussian, so the two learners also face different input distributions. The M1 entry should note this. (5) The MQ learner needs D known in order to sample x_-j ~ D_-j and weight by sqrt(D_j). LGM's setting is distribution-specific with D known, so this is fine, but state it.
- *Feasibility:* The identity checks are feasible: 9 qubits, under a minute. The pipeline comparison and the cost plan are not decisive as written. (a) LGM's own encoding does not produce Fourier samples. A QSQ returns <O>, which for O = QFT^-1 diag(l/M) QFT is the mean of the Fourier distribution, yet v2 eqs. D141-D142 treat the QSQ output as a sample b with |b - kR w*/M1| <= 1/2. My check (q=256, M2=4, uniform D): <O> = 0.423-0.426 for periods S = 3.2-9.1, while the Fourier peaks sit at k*q/S. An 'exact-QSQ pipeline' implemented literally therefore fails, and success criterion (ii) becomes 0 >= 0. Samples must come from projector QSQs with CDF bisection: O(log q) coordinate-local QSQs per sample, still emulable by MQ. (b) Scale. At d = D = 1, faithful LGM parameters give period S = M1/w >= 70*pi ≈ 220 grid points and q >= 3S^2 ≈ 1.45e5 (about 18 qubits per coordinate) plus about 5 label qubits. With 6-7 qubits per coordinate, S <= sqrt(q/3) ≈ 4.6-6.5 and M2 < S/(8*pi) < 1, outside the pseudoperiodicity guarantee. With 3-4 qubits per coordinate at d=2, q = 8-16, so S <= 1.6-2.3 grid points per period: sub-Nyquist, and period recovery is meaningless. (c) Instances. At d=1 the class has one w (known); at d=2 it has 2-3 directions. (d) The success rule is tautological. (i) and the pass branch of (ii) follow from the exact identities plus Hoeffding. The REFUTED branch can fire only through code bugs, or through fragility of the reduced pipeline at tolerance tau, which would hit a finite-shot quantum pipeline equally. The outcome is set by the proof, not the run. (e) The R_w test cannot separate polylog from small-power poly. With R_w in {sqrt(d), d, 2^10}, at d=64 the values of log2 R_w are 3, 6 and 10. Data growing as R_w^0.3 fit an exponent of about 1.2 in log R_w, which passes the '<= 3' rule. (f) The cost of the random-example baselines is underestimated by at least 2 orders of magnitude. Measured HistGradientBoosting fit times: 0.3-0.6 s at N=1e3, d=8; 0.6-1.4 s at N=1e4, d=64. DESIGN §4's 320-config grid x 3 folds is 960 fits, about 5-20 min per cell, over up to 360 cells (6 d x 3 R_w x 5 seeds x 4 N): tens of hours, not 6-8 minutes. Kernel ridge at N=1e5 needs an 80 GB Gram matrix. sin-activation MLPs are not in scikit-learn. DESIGN §4's grids are tuned for AUPRC classification, not squared-loss regression. A pure-Python LLL is only practical for small d.
- *Technical errors:* The hypothesis says LGM separates QSQ from 'classical random-example, gradient or correlational-SQ access'. LGM proves only gradient and correlational-SQ hardness (v2 Thms 1, 2/6). For exact labels, D=1 and gamma = sigma*R_w <= poly(d), the LLL algorithm of 2106.10744 (Thm 3.6) learns cosine neurons from d+1 Gaussian random examples in poly time. So a random-example separation is false for noiseless labels (modulo adapting LLL to LGM's discretized, truncated distribution) and open for labels rounded to 1/M2.; The pipeline's 'two Fourier samples' are undefined in the QSQ model. LGM's observable O = QFT^-1 diag(l/M) QFT returns the mean of the Fourier distribution, and LGM v2 eqs. D141-D142 wrongly treat a QSQ value as a sample. Checked numerically: <O> ≈ 0.424 for every period from 3.2 to 9.1 at q=256. The pipeline must use projector QSQs with CDF bisection, and this LGM gap should itself go into the M1 corrections.; Degenerate test instances. Under LGM's class (B.1 plus the 0.51 net), d=1 has a single, known w = R_w, and the greedy net has 2 points at d=2, 5 at d=3, 10 at d=4 and about 99 at d=8. The 'w-hat recovery' success rate at d <= 2 is therefore not a learning test.; Qubit-scale mismatch. Faithful LGM parameters need q >= 3(70*pi)^2 ≈ 1.45e5 grid points (about 18 qubits) per coordinate even at d = D = 1. The proposed d=2 instances with 3-4 qubits per coordinate have S <= 2.3 grid points per period, which is sub-Nyquist.; 'Both identities hold for ANY labelling, including label noise' is an overclaim. It fails for coherent (QREX-type) label superpositions, whose reduced states contain amplitude cross terms that single MQ answers do not provide.; Part (c) ('the quantum learner gets no period information') holds only for coordinate-local QSQs. A general quantum learner holding the QRAM-loaded state has the full information content of the N samples.; Success-rule (ii) is vacuous if both pipelines fail (0 >= 0). It compares a statistical emulator against exact QSQs rather than against the finite-shot quantum pipeline at the same tau and delta, so a 'refutation' would reflect pipeline fragility, not non-emulability.; Success-rule (iii) cannot distinguish polylog(R_w) from R_w^0.3 on the grid {sqrt(d), d, 2^10}, which is confounded with d.; The cost estimate for the tuned random-example baselines (6-8 min) is off by at least 100x: measured HGB fits take 0.3-1.4 s, x 960 fits per cell, x up to 360 cells.; Inference to verify: the quantum state is on the truncated box, whose mass under LGM's Gaussian is about 0.31^d, while LGM's classical lower bounds are for the untruncated Gaussian. The quantum and classical learners therefore see different distributions.
- *Required fixes:* Reclassify from 'killing experiment' to 'theorem plus verification code'. Pre-register the observable inventory as a reading result: LGM v2 uses only the Fourier observables D19/D22/E39/E70, the verification observable A_m S_{k,-T} (D48-D52), and classical gradient steps. Drop the REFUTED branch, which the run cannot trigger except through bugs.; Rewrite the hypothesis. LGM separates QSQ from gradient and correlational SQ only. Against random examples with exact labels, LLL (2106.10744, Thm 3.6) already matches it for D=1. Against labels rounded to 1/M2, the classical status is open. Against MQ, it matches (this audit).; Repair Fourier sampling. Generate samples with projector QSQs P_{<=y} = QFT^-1 (sum_{l<=y} |l><l|) QFT ⊗ I plus CDF bisection (O(log q) queries per sample), emulate these with the fiber formula too, and record the LGM D141-D142 sample-versus-expectation error as an M1 correction.; Pipeline instances. Use a relaxed continuous w class instead of the 0.51 net (say so explicitly). Run d=1 with 8-9 coordinate qubits (q = 256-512, S ≈ 5-12) and 1-2 label qubits. Keep d=2 for identity checks only.; Success rule. Require that the finite-shot quantum pipeline succeeds on >= 4/5 seeds before any comparison counts. Compare the MQ emulator with the finite-shot quantum pipeline at the same tau and delta (or with identical adversarial perturbations), and report oracle calls on both sides.; Part (b). Specify that the MQ oracle returns h rounded to 1/M2, matching the quantum state's label precision, optionally with tau-bounded adversarial noise. Sweep R_w over 2^4 to 2^40 at fixed d in {8, 64} using exact or high-precision arithmetic. Pre-register a test that rejects any power law in R_w with exponent above about 0.1.; Add LLL as the main random-example baseline for d <= 12, on exact labels and on 1/M2-rounded labels. State that small-d success cannot establish asymptotic hardness either way.; Cut the tuned baselines to a measured budget: d in {4, 16, 64}, R_w = d, N in {1e3, 1e4}, HGB grid of 24 or fewer configs, Nyström kernel ridge (2000 or fewer landmarks), sin-MLP in JAX. Time one cell before pre-registering. Drop the MQ-versus-random-example 'paired' comparison, which is classical versus classical.; Restrict the noise claim to deterministic or independent classical label noise, and restrict part (c) to coordinate-local observables.; Cite 2610.02636 as the methodological precedent (access matching: oracle construction implies MQ) and label novelty as 'application of a known access-matching principle'. Check the truncated-versus-untruncated distribution mismatch in LGM's Appendix C before adding it to M1.
- *Further prior work:* arXiv:2610.02636 (Friedewald, Shiri Sichani, Shyu (Oct 2026), 'Where Quantum Fourier Sampling Stops Short: A Three-Gate Audit Protocol for Delay-PUF Security Models'. Full text read. Publishes the same access-matching principle (building the phase oracle implies classical membership access; Kushilevitz-Mansour as the matched baseline) for Boolean Fourier sampling, and finds no end-to-end advantage. Does not treat LGM.); arXiv:2106.10744 (Already cited, but used too weakly; read Secs 1-3 and App D. Thm 3.6: LLL learns cos(2*pi*gamma<w,x>) from d+1 noiseless Gaussian examples in poly(d) time for 1 <= gamma <= d^Q, beta <= exp(-(d log d)^3). This undercuts the hypothesis' claim that LGM separates from random-example access when labels are exact. Cryptographic hardness needs beta = 1/poly and gamma >= 2*sqrt(d).); arXiv:2602.01054 (Already cited; selected pages read. Thm 3.1: QEX cannot simulate QMEM, so the argument must rest on realizability (oracle construction implies MQ), not on QEX and MQ being 'the same oracle'.); arXiv:2503.20879 (v2 full-text pages read (main text, App A, C1-C2 partial, D1, E1). New points: (a) 'only one type of query is not a correlational QSQ'; (b) eqs. D141-D142 treat a QSQ value as a Fourier sample, which my numerical check shows is wrong; (c) the 0.51 net makes the class tiny at small d.)

## T5. Learning observables and physical labels from classical inputs

### I-20261012-05: Exponential sample advantage in learning quantum observables

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | external batch A |
| Hypothesis | A quantum learner predicts expectation values of a family of observables from classical data with exponentially fewer samples than any classical learner, on a natural distribution. |
| Mechanism | Molteni, Gyurik & Dunjko establish exponential advantages for learning observables from classical data; the observable is evaluated coherently. |
| Data | Classical n-bit inputs; labels = <O> on a state prepared from the input. |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | n=4 (16 possible inputs), shallow U(x), O=Z1Z2+X3, N=200 samples; parameterized-circuit learner vs kernel ridge regression, MLP, random forest; metric: MSE; success: quantum MSE<=0.01 with <=200 samples while classical need >=2000 or plateau >0.05. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | Asymptotic; observable family may be engineered. |
| References cited | 2405.02027, 2011.01938, 2010.02174, 2306.16028 |
| Reference checks | 2405.02027: **CLAIM NOT IN PAPER** (Paper exists (Molteni, Gyurik, Dunjko); main text read. It proves a TIME-complexity separation. Sec. II states its results 'focus primarily on the time efficiency of the learning algorithm', in contrast to sample-complexity work. Hardness is worst-case and distribution-free (Def. 4: any input distribution), not 'on a natural distribution'. The quantum learner uses N = O(poly(n) log(poly(n)/delta)/eps^2) samples (Thm 3), the same order a computationally unbounded classical learner would need. 'Exponentially fewer samples' is not claimed.); 2011.01938: **OK** (Exists (Huang et al., Power of data). No specific claim is attributed to it. It actually cuts against the idea: classical learners with data can be competitive, and advantages appear only on engineered data.); 2010.02174: **OK** (Exists (Liu, Arunachalam, Temme). Discrete-log separation with a fault-tolerant kernel. Background only; it is not about observables or sample counts.); 2306.16028: **OK** (Exists (Gyurik, Dunjko). Background on evaluation vs identification hardness; it is the heuristic-hardness predecessor that Molteni et al. improve on. Supports no sample-advantage claim.) |
| Technical problems | Sample vs time: in Molteni et al.'s setting both learners receive the same classical dataset {(x, y)}. The gap is the cost of evaluating Tr[U\|x><x\|U^dag P_j], not the number of samples. Huang-Kueng-Preskill (2101.02464, Thm 1) also show that for average-case squared error (the idea's MSE metric) a classical learner needs at most O(m N_Q/eps) runs of the process. An exponential sample advantage in MSE is impossible here.; 'Natural distribution' is wrong. The hardness requires learning under every distribution, and BQP not in P/poly gives no hardness for any fixed natural D (that needs heuristic assumptions as in 2306.16028).; Lookup table solves the minimal test. n=4 gives 16 inputs; with 200 i.i.d. uniform draws, P(some input unseen) <= 16(15/16)^200 = 4e-5 (about 54 draws suffice on average). A table, or exact 16-coefficient Walsh regression, reaches label-noise-level MSE, so 'classical needs >= 2000 samples or plateaus > 0.05' cannot occur.; Ill-posed concept. O = Z1Z2 + X3 is fixed and U is known, so the target is fully determined and nothing is learned. If U is unknown and fitted by a 'parameterised circuit', the model is a VQC (K3) with no link to Molteni's LASSO-on-Pauli-features learner.; 'Shallow U' with bounded local norm has a light cone. f(x) is a sum of a few local juntas, and classical local regression learns it. In our 8-qubit check at tau=0.25, 98.4% of f's Walsh variance sits on subsets spanning <= 2 sites, and black-box local-Walsh LASSO matched the U-aware model (relative MSE 0.014 vs 0.013 at N=64).; Unfair baselines (K4). KRR/MLP/RF are denied the description of U, which the PAC setting gives both learners. The fair classical twin computes the same Pauli features by exact simulation and is identical to the quantum model at n <= 10.; K5: the asymptotic claim needs BQP-hard (poly(n) norm x time) evolution estimated to 1/poly precision, i.e. fault tolerance. With constant gate noise, expectation values on computational-basis inputs are classically simulable on average (2407.12768). |
| Kill-criteria check | K6, K7, K4, K5 |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2405.02027 (Proves the computational separation the idea restates; quantum learner is Pauli features plus LASSO; hard instance is a poly(n)-norm Feynman clock.); arXiv:2101.02464 (Average-case (MSE) sample advantage of any quantum learner is at most polynomial; exponential gaps only for worst-case error.); arXiv:2607.06472 (Follow-up separation for predicting time evolution; needs poly(n)-long times and says the advantage comes from evaluation, not identification.); arXiv:2506.17089 (Separation for Hamiltonian dynamics with O(log n) unknown parameters via Fourier coefficient extraction; no tuned-baseline benchmark.) |
| Status | **REJECTED** |
| Decision note | The cited separation (2405.02027) is computational, not sample-based. It assumes BQP not in P/poly, is distribution-free, and uses a poly(n)-norm Feynman-clock Hamiltonian; both learners use poly(n) samples. At n=4 (16 inputs) or any n <= 10 a classical learner reproduces the quantum features exactly, so the minimal test cannot show the claimed effect, and the theory itself is already proven. |

### I-20261012-20: Learning physical observables from classically labelled dynamics

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | external batch C |
| Hypothesis | A quantum predictor incorporating known local evolution learns an unknown observable from fewer labelled bitstrings than tuned RBF-SVM, MLP, GBT and sparse regression. |
| Mechanism | Labels y = Tr[U\|x><x\|U^dag O*] + noise with U local non-Clifford evolution and O* an unknown sum of low-weight Paulis; the quantum model uses the generating mechanism. |
| Data | Classical (bitstring, expectation value) pairs. |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | 6-8-qubit spin chains; trainable observable after the physical circuit vs tuned RBF-SVM, MLP, GBT, sparse regression on physically motivated features; held-out bitstrings and Hamiltonian parameters; RMSE vs labels and label shots. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | At 6-8 qubits classical simulation makes the features accessible; tests finite-size sample efficiency only. |
| References cited | 2405.02027, 2011.01938 |
| Reference checks | 2405.02027: **OK** (The setting matches the paper (eq. 3 concept class; Algorithm 1 LASSO on Pauli features of U\|x>). Caveat: the paper's separation is computational and distribution-free, and its hard H has poly(n) norm (App. A, Lemma 1). It does not claim fewer labelled samples than black-box learners, and it does not cover bounded-norm 'local non-Clifford' constant-time evolution.); 2011.01938: **OK** (Exists; relevant background (classical learners with data can match quantum models; geometric-difference test). No specific claim attributed.) |
| Technical problems | The hypothesis compares learners with different information (U known vs unknown). Under the project's fairness rule this is an arbitrary handicap: U's description is classical and available to both learners in the PAC formulation. At 6-8 qubits the fair classical twin (exact-simulation features + the same LASSO) has zero difference by construction.; Bounded-strength local evolution for O(1) time is classically learnable. By Lieb-Robinson bounds, f(x) = sum of m functions of O(v*tau + log(m/eps)) bits, so local regression learns it with poly(n) * 2^{O(v*tau)} samples. Molteni's hardness uses a poly(n)-norm clock Hamiltonian, and 2607.06472 needs poly(n) times. Our 8-qubit check (mixed-field Ising, 6-sparse O over 87 one-site/nearest-neighbour Paulis): at tau=0.25 black-box local-Walsh LASSO ties the U-aware model (0.014 vs 0.013 relative MSE at N=64); at tau=1, 98% of Walsh variance has span <= 3.; The gap at long times is prior knowledge, not quantumness. At tau=4, black boxes fail (local-Walsh LASSO 0.54, RBF-KRR 0.65, GBT 0.69 relative MSE at N=64; 0.48/0.58/0.59 at N=160) while U-aware LASSO reaches 0.008/0.003. A classical model holding U gets exactly the same numbers.; Finite-size only. 64-256 inputs; any target is a 2^n-coefficient Walsh polynomial, so the possible sample gap is bounded by roughly 2^n/(m log m) and says nothing asymptotic. The card's own 'risk' line concedes this.; 'Held-out Hamiltonian parameters': a model that simulates the new H extrapolates trivially and a black box cannot. This is again prior knowledge.; K5: feature shot cost (N points x m Paulis x R shots, plus errors-in-variables bias in LASSO) is not budgeted, only label shots. On noisy hardware, bitstring-input expectation values are classically simulable on average (2407.12768). |
| Kill-criteria check | K6, K7, K4, K5 |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2405.02027 (Identical model (Pauli features of known evolution + LASSO) and setting; computational separation only.); arXiv:2504.16370 (Feature-plus-linear-regression pipeline for Tr[f(H)rho] run on 12-40 superconducting qubits; labels computed classically by TEBD, no feature-free classical baseline, hardness only argued.); arXiv:2601.22006 (Quantum-computed expectation values as privileged training features; Rydberg-chain numerics with SVM/SVM+/transformer baselines; gains only in the boundary-sampled, 15-40 point regime.); arXiv:2210.14894 (Classical ML learns arbitrary-process outputs on average for locally flat input distributions (not computational-basis inputs); numerics to 50 qubits.); arXiv:2607.06472 (Separation for predicting time evolution needs poly(n)-long times; advantage from evaluation, not identification.) |
| Status | **REJECTED** |
| Decision note | This is Molteni et al.'s Algorithm 1 (trainable observable = LASSO on Pauli features of a known evolution) run at 6-8 qubits against black-box regressors that are denied U. Any win comes from prior knowledge of U, not from quantumness: a classical learner given the same U computes identical features exactly. The asymptotic separation needs BQP-hard, poly(n)-time dynamics that cannot be emulated at <= 10 qubits, and feature-plus-regression pipelines are already demonstrated (2504.16370 on 40 qubits; 2601.22006). |

*No best version proposed for T5.* Nothing in T5 deserves an experiment in this repository. Both ideas restate the separation of Molteni, Gyurik & Dunjko (2405.02027). Read in full, that separation is computational and distribution-free. The quantum learner computes the expectation values of m Pauli strings on U|x> and fits an l1-bounded LASSO; the classical learner fails only because evaluating those features is BQP-hard. The hard instance is a Feynman-clock Hamiltonian whose norm grows as poly(n) (App. A, Lemma 1). It is not a sample-complexity separation. For the MSE metric both ideas use, Huang-Kueng-Preskill (2101.02464, Thm 1) cap any sample advantage at a polynomial. The theme in four regimes (verified sources plus our own check): - Bitstring inputs with bounded-strength local H for short times: classically learnable through the light cone. This is folklore; we found no paper stating it for Molteni's concept class. - Locally flat random product inputs with any process: classically learnable on average (2210.14894). - Average-case sample counts: the quantum advantage is at most polynomial (2101.02464). - Separations exist only for evaluation-hard dynamics with poly(n) norm or time (2405.02027, 2506.17089, 2607.06472). In practice they need fault tolerance, because noisy circuits on computational-basis inputs are classically simulable on average (2407.12768). None of these regimes can show a quantum effect at <= 10 qubits: a classical learner given U simulates the features exactly. Our check is the scratch script /tmp/claude-0/-home-user-quantum-ml-reality-check/3242e8f8-b193-546c-b381-3fe888e7979d/scratchpad/round01/t5/t5_check.py. Setup: 8 qubits, mixed-field Ising H with J=1, hx=0.9, hz=0.5; target O a 6-sparse combination of 87 one-site and nearest-neighbour Paulis; label noise sigma=0.02; held-out relative MSE, 3 draws; small tuning grids, so this is a sanity check, not DESIGN.md §4 tuning. Results: - At short times (tau=0.25) a black-box local-Walsh LASSO ties the U-aware model. - At long times (tau=4) the black boxes fail (0.48-0.69) while the U-aware model reaches 0.003-0.008. The whole gap is knowing U, which is not a quantum resource. Suggested FIELD_MAP updates (no idea needed): 1. M1, Molteni et al.: add that the separation is computational and distribution-free, that the 'constant-time' hard Hamiltonian has poly(n) norm, and that the quantum learner is Pauli features plus LASSO. 2. Candidate new M1/M8 sources: 2607.06472, 2506.17089, 2504.16370 and 2601.22006. 2504.16370's 40-qubit hardware demonstration has R^2 < 0 at 40 qubits, and its labels came from classical TEBD, so its instances are classically tractable. If the project wants a positive control that the §6 rule can register a win, the 'U-aware features vs black box' construction would do. It belongs in T6, labelled explicitly as a prior-knowledge advantage, not a quantum one. The coordinator's flagged problems (2407.07754, 2111.05451, 2608.13521) are not cited by either T5 idea. 2405.02027's journal version (npj Quant. Inf. 12, 19 (2026)) is only cited by 2607.06472: NOT VERIFIED. arxiv_search.py calls used: 4 of 6.
