## T6. Structured or symmetric quantum kernels on classical data, and a positive control

### I-20261012-04: Escaping kernel concentration with symmetry-structured bandwidth

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | external batch A |
| Hypothesis | A quantum kernel with a data-dependent bandwidth structured by the data's symmetry group reaches geometric difference g ~ sqrt(N) on symmetric data and beats a tuned RBF-SVM. |
| Mechanism | Use sector-wise bandwidths respecting a permutation/rotation symmetry to keep expressivity without concentration; claims a classical kernel cannot imitate it because the symmetry lives in the embedding circuit. |
| Data | Classical synthetic data from a symmetry-invariant function with label noise. |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | 6 qubits, symmetry-respecting angle encoding, exact kernel, separate bandwidths per symmetry sector; baselines: RBF-SVM, polynomial SVM, GBT; metrics: test accuracy and g; success: +5 pp over best classical and g>=10, with an unentangled ablation. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | Kernel may still collapse to a classical one (Bowles et al.). |
| References cited | 2208.11060, 2111.05451 (attributed to 'Canatar et al.'), 2403.07059 |
| Reference checks | 2208.11060: **OK** (Thanasilp, Wang, Cerezo, Holmes, 'Exponential concentration in quantum kernel methods'. Existence verified (table and arXiv API); cited generically for concentration, which matches the paper (FIELD_MAP M6 [F]). Not re-opened in this task.); 2111.05451 (attributed to 'Canatar et al.'): **MISATTRIBUTED** (2111.05451 is Shaydulin & Wild, 'Importance of Kernel Bandwidth in Quantum Machine Learning'. The Canatar et al. bandwidth paper is 2206.06686 (Canatar, Peters, Pehlevan, Wild, Shaydulin), verified via the arXiv API, abstract read.); 2403.07059: **OK** (Bowles, Ahmed, Schuld exist as cited. Per FIELD_MAP M5 [F] (not re-opened here), classical baselines systematically outperform and removing entanglement rarely hurts. 'The kernel collapses to a classical one' is better supported by 2211.16551 and 2503.05602, which I read.) |
| Technical problems | Wrong premise: symmetry is not a quantum resource. If rho(gx) = U_g rho(x) U_g^dag, the G-invariant (twirled) quantum kernel equals mean_g k_Q(x, gx'), the same group average that turns an RBF into a G-invariant RBF.; Labels from 'a symmetry-invariant function with label noise' are classical, so the Bayes-optimal classifier is a classical invariant function.; 'Sector-wise bandwidths' is a multi-bandwidth (ARD-like) kernel. A fair classical twin needs the same per-sector length scales (K4). Per-bandwidth tuning moves quantum kernels towards RBF/polynomial behaviour (2503.05602; 2211.16551).; The success criterion is inconsistent: the hypothesis asks for g ~ sqrt(N), the test asks for g >= 10, and N is never fixed.; g against non-invariant classical kernels is inflated by the symmetry alone. Scratch S_6 check, s = 0.7: g(RBF\|\|Q) = 3.7 rose to g(RBF\|\|Q^G) = 5.9 after symmetrising the quantum kernel only. Against the symmetrised RBF it was 5.0, and all values were << sqrt(N) = 17.3. g must be computed against group-averaged classical kernels.; A finite-sample g is not a reliable measure of advantage anyway: it is conditioning-sensitive (2610.11360, 2610.02636). |
| Kill-criteria check | K1: the claimed edge comes from the data's symmetry and from sector-wise bandwidths. Any classical kernel gets both, by group averaging k_G(x,x') = mean_g k(x,gx'), by invariant features or canonicalisation, and by per-sector length scales (ARD). The labels are a classical invariant function, so nothing in the task is quantum., K4: the baselines (RBF, polynomial SVM, GBT) are not invariant. A 'win' would measure symmetry versus no symmetry, not quantum versus classical., K7 (partial): bandwidth tuning is already known to drive quantum kernels towards RBF/polynomial kernels and small g (2211.16551, 2503.05602). |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2211.16551 (Bandwidth-tuned IQP fidelity kernels: g against classical kernels decays to well below sqrt(N).); arXiv:2503.05602 (Optimally bandwidth-tuned FQKs and PQKs resemble RBF and low-order polynomial kernels.); arXiv:2509.14337 (Covariant (coset) kernels avoid concentration, but no learning experiments and no classical baselines.); arXiv:2608.29422 (Abstract: at small bandwidth the ZZ kernel equals a parameter-free Gaussian surrogate, isotropic under the unshifted phase convention.) |
| Status | **REJECTED** |
| Decision note | Rejected under K1 and K4. The mechanism ('a classical kernel cannot imitate it because the symmetry lives in the embedding circuit') is false. For a covariant embedding, twirling the quantum kernel is exactly the same group average that any classical kernel can use, and the labels come from a classical invariant function. The baselines are non-invariant. In a scratch S_6 check (6 qubits, one seed, 100 test points), the permutation-averaged IQP kernel scored 0.80 and beat plain RBF (0.76), but lost to the permutation-averaged RBF (0.86) and to RBF on sorted inputs (0.81). The quantum arm is kept as one arm of I-20261012-37. |

### I-20261012-14: Group-invariant projected quantum kernels via subspace projection

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | external batch B |
| Hypothesis | A group-invariant projected quantum kernel beats classical RBF-SVMs on permutation/rotation-symmetric tasks without exponential concentration. |
| Mechanism | Projection onto a low-dimensional invariant subspace avoids concentration while keeping g above classical bounds. |
| Data | Classical structured data (8-d point clouds with rotation symmetry). |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | 8-qubit projected symmetric kernel SVM vs RBF-SVM and equivariant GNNs; metrics: accuracy and g; success: higher accuracy with g > sqrt(N). |
| Main risk (as proposed) | Projection may make the kernel classically simulable via invariant polynomial features. |
| References cited | 2208.11060, 2111.05451, 2312.09121 |
| Reference checks | 2208.11060: **OK** (Exists; cited for concentration, consistent with the title and FIELD_MAP M6 [F].); 2111.05451: **OK** (Shaydulin & Wild, bandwidth tuning. Correctly cited here (no author attribution).); 2312.09121: **OK** (Cerezo et al., 'Does provable absence of barren plateaus imply classical simulability?'. Used for the simulability risk, which is appropriate. It is a perspective with case-by-case evidence, not a theorem (FIELD_MAP M4 [F]).) |
| Technical problems | The card gives no rotation-covariant qubit encoding. The IQP map is covariant under permutations of features and qubits but not under rotations of the input vectors. An SO(2)-covariant encoding needs, for example, polar angles as RZ phases. The resulting kernel depends on relative angles, which a classical kernel on relative angles reproduces.; 'Keeping g above classical bounds' is unsupported. Measured against non-invariant classical kernels, g grows from the invariance alone (see the I-04 scratch check). Against group-averaged kernels there is no reason for g > sqrt(N).; '8-d point clouds' is ambiguous (4 points in 2D? 8 scalars?), and the symmetry group (SO(2) x S_4?) is not stated. |
| Kill-criteria check | K1 (standalone): projecting onto a low-dimensional invariant subspace gives a kernel that depends on at most d^2 invariant expectation values for a d-dimensional subspace. A classical model on invariant features can copy that inductive bias, as the card itself suspects., K4 (standalone): 'g > sqrt(N)' and accuracy gains measured against a non-invariant RBF-SVM confound symmetry with quantumness. The equivariant-GNN baseline is right in spirit, but the decisive baselines are the group-averaged RBF and invariant features + GBT. |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2105.03406 (Covariant quantum kernels for group-structured data, 27 qubits; no classical baseline in the pages read.); arXiv:2509.14337 (Concentration-free covariant kernels; kernel values only, no learning.); arXiv:2602.02008 (In the QSQ model, symmetry gives a quantum advantage only for special classes and skewed orbit distributions.) |
| Status | **MERGED into I-20261012-37** |
| Decision note | Same claim as I-04 (symmetry in the embedding gives an advantage), so it trips K1/K4 on its own. Its usable parts move to I-37: the projected group-invariant quantum kernel as a second quantum arm, and the invariant/equivariant classical baselines. There, the fair comparison is against group-averaged classical kernels, with g computed against them. |

### I-20261012-37: Is symmetry a quantum resource? Equivariant quantum kernels vs group-averaged classical kernels

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | Claude |
| Hypothesis | On data with an exact group symmetry, a symmetry-respecting quantum kernel does not beat a classical RBF kernel averaged over the same group, k_G(x,x') = mean_g k(x, g x'). |
| Mechanism | Several proposals (ideas 04, 14) credit symmetry to the quantum embedding; the fair baseline injects the same symmetry classically. This sharpens kill criterion K1 for symmetry-based kernels. |
| Data | Classical synthetic data with S_n or rotation symmetry. |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | 6-8 qubits; equivariant quantum kernel vs RBF, group-averaged RBF, invariant features + GBT; success (quantum): beats the group-averaged kernel under section 6 with large g; expected: no. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | A negative result by design; may duplicate known invariant-kernel theory. |
| References cited | 2205.06217 |
| Reference checks | 2205.06217: **OK** (Meyer et al. (partial text read). Symmetrised variational models beat non-invariant quantum models in generalisation. They compare only quantum against quantum and name comparison with classical models as future work, which supports the gap this idea fills.) |
| Technical problems | The card does not say the finite-sample geometric difference is not monotone under symmetrisation. The claim has to be stated for RKHS norms of invariant targets (population level) and tested with learners.; Averaging over S_8 (40,320 permutations) at 8 qubits and N = 2,000 is too costly for the 10-minute budget. Use S_6 at 6 qubits, or C_8/D_8 at 8 qubits, or canonicalisation.; For S_n the classical canonicalisation baseline (sorting) is a different invariant model from group averaging. Both should be included, since either can win. |
| Kill-criteria check | K7 (considered, not tripped): the classical theory of group-averaged (Haar-integration) invariant kernels is standard; no specific reference was verified in this task (NOT VERIFIED). The quantum-specific statement, and the protocol rule it implies for this repository, add testable content., K1 (considered, not tripped): the idea is a sharpening of K1, not a claim of advantage., K6 (not tripped): 6 qubits (S_6, 720 permutations) or 8 qubits with the cyclic group C_8 and a ring-pattern IQP map run exactly on a laptop. |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2205.06217 (Invariant against non-invariant quantum models only; calls for a classical comparison.); arXiv:2602.02008 (QSQ: for most symmetry groups, quantum and classical SQ lower bounds coincide.); arXiv:2211.16551 (States that group-invariant QML 'applies trivially to quantum kernels'.); arXiv:2503.05465 (A permutation-invariant quantum kernel claimed to beat classical kernel learning; an instance of the confound this test controls for (abstract only).) |
| Status | **SHORTLISTED (weakened in adversarial review: apply the required fixes before building)** |
| Decision note | Worth doing, cheaply, as theory plus a killing experiment. It turns the objection to I-04 and I-14 into a written, checkable statement and a fixed baseline rule. Projection lemma (my derivation, to be written up and checked): let k satisfy k(gx,gx') = k(x,x'), which holds for RBF under orthogonal actions and for any covariant quantum embedding. Then k^G(x,x') = mean_g k(x,gx') has RKHS P_G H_k with the inherited norm, where P_G is the orthogonal projection onto G-invariant functions. The twirled covariant quantum kernel is exactly k_Q^G. So for every G-invariant target f, the RKHS norms with and without symmetrisation are equal, and the classical-to-quantum norm ratio (Huang's model-complexity ratio in the population limit) is unchanged. Symmetrisation gives both sides the same gain and cannot create an advantage the base kernels lack. Caveat: the finite-sample regularised g is not monotone under symmetrisation (scratch check: g(RBF^G\|\|Q^G) = 2.8, 5.0, 6.1 against g(RBF\|\|Q) = 2.4, 3.7, 3.2 at s = 0.3, 0.7, 1.0, all << sqrt(N) = 17.3). The empirical arm must therefore compare learners, not only g. |
| Decisive test | (a) Write the one-page proof of the projection lemma and its corollary for covariant quantum kernels; check it numerically: for an invariant target, the RKHS norm and the kernel-ridge complexity are unchanged by group averaging. (b) Under DESIGN.md section 6 (5 seeds, same rows, splits and folds), run 6 qubits on an S_6-invariant synthetic task, or 8 qubits with C_8 and a ring IQP map, with labels from a fixed invariant function plus 10% flips. Compare the Q^G fidelity and Q^G projected SVMs (bandwidth grid as in creditcard_q8) against the RBF^G-SVM (same gamma x C grid), RBF on canonicalised inputs (sorted features for S_n, cyclic shift to the max-first coordinate for C_n), GBT on invariant features (sorted values / power sums), and the unsymmetrised RBF and Q as references. Report g against group-averaged classical kernels. Pre-registered: a symmetric quantum kernel 'wins' only if it beats the RBF^G-SVM under section 6. Expected: no clear difference or a classical win. Protocol rule if confirmed: every future symmetric-kernel proposal is benchmarked against the group-averaged classical kernel. Cost: about 120 lines, under 10 CPU-minutes (the scratch S_6 version took 6.5 min for one seed with all g computations; the learner-only runs are faster). |

### I-20261012-33: Positive control: engineered labels where the quantum kernel must win

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | Claude |
| Hypothesis | On labels constructed from the quantum kernel's own geometry (Huang et al.'s construction), the quantum kernel SVM beats every tuned classical model in our pipeline, and the gap shrinks as the geometric difference g shrinks. |
| Mechanism | Huang et al. pick labels along directions where K_Q generalises and every classical kernel cannot (top eigenvector of sqrt(K_Q) K_C^-1 sqrt(K_Q)); by construction a classical kernel cannot copy them. This calibrates whether our benchmark can detect an advantage when one exists, which makes its negative results more credible. |
| Data | Classical inputs (our fraud features or synthetic), engineered labels. |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | 8 qubits, bandwidths s in {0.1, 0.3, 1}; compute g, build labels, run the creditcard_q8 pipeline with all tuned baselines; success: quantum wins under DESIGN.md section 6 where g is large, and the win disappears as g falls (dose-response). |
| Main risk (as proposed) | Engineered labels: no useful advantage (by design); K7, Huang et al. did it at larger scale; at 8 qubits g may stay small. |
| References cited | 2011.01938 |
| Reference checks | 2011.01938: **OK** (Exists and contains the construction (App. G, App. L eqs L14-L15; read in partial full text). The card's description needs correcting: the labels are y = sqrt(K_Q) v, not v, with v the top eigenvector of the regularised matrix sqrt(K_Q) sqrt(K_C)(K_C+lam I)^-2 sqrt(K_C) sqrt(K_Q). K_C is the classical kernel with the smallest g, and the labels are binarised by sign with 10% flips or at the median. Huang et al. report advantage mainly for projected kernels (E3). Engineered sets for the original fidelity kernel showed no substantial advantage at large n (Fig. 6).) |
| Technical problems | The mechanism as written ('top eigenvector of sqrt(K_Q) K_C^-1 sqrt(K_Q)') omits the regularisation and the sqrt(K_Q) mapping (labels y = sqrt(K_Q) v), and does not say how labels are binarised.; 'By construction a classical kernel cannot copy them' holds only for the single minimising kernel in the g list, and only at the level of a bound. The repository's RBF-SVM grid includes gamma factors (0.1 x scale) outside Huang's g list (0.25-64), and GBT and MLP are not covered at all. Construct the labels against the union of all classical kernels used, and report.; The in-sample construction uses the test inputs (transductive). Use the out-of-sample variant of 2610.11360: direction from the training split, test labels from the kernel expansion.; The pre-registered dose-response on g is not well founded. Finite-sample g is conditioning-sensitive (2610.11360: g fell 5.6x with unchanged advantage; 2610.02636: g grows through ill-conditioning). In the scratch pilot the gap was not monotone in g (fidelity s = 1: g 3.5, gap +0.2-0.27; projected s = 1: g 10.8, gap +0.05-0.14). Keep g as a covariate and test whether it predicts the gap.; At s = 1 the fidelity kernel is at the onset of concentration (READING: mean kernel value 0.11 at 10 qubits). The win may not survive finite shots (K5), so a shot-noise arm is needed.; Labels are balanced, so the AUPRC baseline is 0.5. Keep AUPRC for the pipeline but also report accuracy for comparison with Huang and Kar. |
| Kill-criteria check | K7 (considered, not tripped): Huang et al. (2011.01938) built these engineered datasets (up to 30 qubits). They were repeated on neutral-atom hardware (2407.02553), with photonic kernels and full controls (2610.11360), and as semi-artificial relabelling of real tabular data (2508.19437). This is a replication of a known separation. What it adds here: this repository's inputs, its tuned GradBoost/MLP/RBF/LogReg grids and the paired section-6 rule, and a direct test of the repository's own reading of g., K4 (not tripped): the full tuned creditcard_q8 classical grids are used., K5 (addressed): the strongest dose sits where concentration starts (fidelity kernel at s = 1), so a finite-shot arm is required., K6 (not tripped): 8 qubits, exact simulation; pilot cost measured. |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2011.01938 (Original engineered datasets: projected kernel >20% ahead up to 30 qubits; fidelity-kernel version shows no large-n advantage.); arXiv:2610.11360 (Same protocol for photonic kernels, with out-of-sample, reverse and random-label controls: designed +0.22, natural ~0; g does not track the advantage.); arXiv:2407.02553 (Hardware (neutral atoms, up to 108 qubits) 'comparative kernel advantage' on g-based synthetic datasets (abstract).); arXiv:2508.19437 (Semi-artificial relabelling of real tabular data favouring a quantum kernel; RBF-only baseline.); arXiv:2211.16551 (Label-agnostic: tuned bandwidth gives small g, so the positive control must also cover the tuned-bandwidth regime.) |
| Status | **SHORTLISTED (weakened in adversarial review: apply the required fixes before building)** |
| Decision note | Shortlisted as calibration (replication of a known separation). It is the only idea in the theme that can change how the repository's negative results are read. A scratch pilot (synthetic Gaussian inputs, 8 qubits, N = 800, 2 seeds) shows the construction works at 8 qubits but contradicts the card's dose-response assumption. The fidelity IQP kernel at s = 1 had g of only 3.4-3.6 (<< sqrt(N) = 28.3), yet the quantum SVM beat tuned RBF-SVM, GBT and MLP by +0.20 to +0.27 accuracy. The projected kernel at s = 1 had g = 10.8 but a smaller gap (+0.05 to +0.14). At s <= 0.3 (g 1.5-3.6) there was no gap. If this holds on the fraud inputs, it undercuts the sentence in READING.md section 4 and FIELD_MAP M2 that g ~ 1.3-4.9 << sqrt(N) guarantees a classical kernel predicts 'about as well for any labels'. |
| Decisive test | See best_version. In short: out-of-sample engineered labels on the creditcard_q8 inputs, 6 doses (fidelity/projected IQP x s0 in {0.1, 0.3, 1}), plus reverse-construction, random-label and separable-twin controls, with the full tuned grids and the section-6 rule. Pre-registered checks: P1 sensitivity, P2 specificity, P3 whether g predicts the gap, P4 the finite-shot arm. |

### T6 best version: Positive control with out-of-sample engineered labels: can our benchmark detect a built-in quantum-kernel advantage, and does g predict it? (replication of a known separation: Huang et al. 2011.01938 App. G)

*Kind:* calibration / infrastructure. *Built from:* I-20261012-33.

- **Hypothesis:** The pipeline under test: the creditcard_q8 inputs (8 PCA features; 1,400 train / 600 test; 5 seeds; same folds), the full tuned grids and the DESIGN.md section-6 rule. If the labels are engineered from a quantum kernel's geometry, the generating quantum SVM beats every tuned classical model for at least one dose. Random labels give 'no clear difference'. Labels built adversarially to the quantum kernel give a classical win. The secondary hypothesis, which the scratch pilot suggests is false, is that the label-agnostic g of geometry.py predicts the size of the gap.
- **Mechanism:** Huang's bound: prediction error <= c sqrt(s_K/N). Labels that solve max s_C/s_Q (the generalised eigenproblem of App. G) give s_C = g^2 s_Q against the minimising classical kernel, so classical kernel methods need about g^2 times more data. Whether trees, MLPs and kernels outside the g list are also hampered is empirical (Huang reports it is robust). Note that s_C <= g^2 s_Q only bounds the classical error within a factor g of the quantum bound. When s_Q/N is not small, a g of 3-5 still leaves room for large gaps, which is what the pilot saw. At 8 qubits K_Q is computed classically, so any win is an inductive-bias match by construction, not a quantum advantage (DESIGN.md section 6, last paragraph).
- **Data:** Inputs: the creditcard_q8 rows of each seed (make_split: 2,000 rows, 8 PCA components re-standardised, 70/30 split), with the fraud labels discarded. Engineered labels, out-of-sample variant (2610.11360). (1) On the training split, compute the trace-normalised K_Q and the classical family F = {linear} U {Gaussian gamma = f/(n Var), f in Huang's list U the RBF-SVM grid factors}. (2) Choose (K_C, lam) = argmin g over F x LAMBDAS with g_tra < 0.045 (geometry.py rule). (3) Take v = top eigenvector of sqrt(K_Q) sqrt(K_C)(K_C+lam I)^-2 sqrt(K_C) sqrt(K_Q) and set y'_tr = sqrt(K_Q) v. (4) Define the label function f(x) = sum_i alpha_i k_Q(x, x_i) with alpha = K_Q^+ y'_tr (eigenvalue cutoff 1e-8). (5) Label all train and test points by sign(f(x) - median_train f), then flip 10% at random with a fixed seed (Bayes accuracy 0.90). Six doses: generating kernel in {IQP fidelity, IQP projected with gamma-factor 1} x s0 in {0.1, 0.3, 1.0}. Controls: (C1) reverse construction, with the roles of K_Q and the tuned RBF kernel swapped; (C2) balanced random labels; (C3) the separable twin (pattern=[]) learning the entangled-kernel labels. CV folds are re-stratified on the engineered labels, identically for all models.
- **Quantum model:** The creditcard_q8 quantum SVMs, unchanged: QSVM-fidelity (IQPEmbedding(s*x), 2 repeats, 96 configs) and QSVM-projected (256 configs). Both bandwidth grids contain the generating s0. Plus their separable twins (control C3). Exact statevector kernels (qmlrc/quantum.py). P4 arm: finite-shot kernels at R in {1,000; 10,000} via qmlrc/shots.py, with labels from the exact kernel and learning from the noisy kernel, for the strongest dose. No VQC (not part of the label construction; too costly).
- **Classical baselines:** The tuned creditcard_q8 grids, unchanged: LogReg (16), RBF-SVM (80), HistGradientBoosting (320), MLP (12). Two new classical baselines with at least as many configurations as the quantum SVMs. (i) IQP-angle surrogate: an RBF-SVM on the 72 features [cos, sin] of the IQP angles (s x_i, s^2 x_i x_j), with s over the quantum bandwidth grid. This tests whether the edge is 'the feature set' rather than interference. (ii) The parameter-free Gaussian surrogate of the ZZ map (2608.29422, abstract level; implement from the stated metric I + pi^2 Q, isotropic under PennyLane's unshifted convention) with C and class_weight tuned. All models use the same rows, folds and CV metric.
- **Access model:** Classical data only. Both learners receive the same 1,400 labelled training pairs and are tested on the same 600 points. The quantum learner gets exact kernel entries; in P4, R shots per fidelity entry or 3 measurement bases x R shots per point for the projected kernel. The classical learners get raw features and grids at least as large. Asymmetry stated plainly: the label function lies in the generating quantum kernel's RKHS by design, so the quantum model's prior is matched. A classical learner that simulated the same 8-qubit circuit would tie. This is a calibration of sensitivity and specificity, never evidence of quantum advantage.
- **Metric:** Primary: test AUPRC (the pipeline metric; balanced labels, so the baseline is 0.5), decided pair by pair with DESIGN.md section 6 (higher on >= 4/5 seeds and mean paired difference > SD). Secondary: test accuracy; per-dose mean g (geometry.py) and the label-aware kernel-target alignment gap dA = A(K_Q,y) - max_C A(K_C,y) (2610.11360), both reported as covariates.
- **Success rule:** Pre-registered before any fraud-input run. P1 (sensitivity), the positive control passes if: for at least one dose, the generating QSVM beats every classical model, both surrogates included, under section 6. P2 (specificity): in C2 every quantum/classical pair is 'no clear difference', and in C1 the tuned RBF-SVM beats the generating QSVM under section 6. The benchmark is calibrated only if P1 and P2 both hold. P3 (validity of g): g is 'predictive' if Spearman rho(mean g, mean paired gap) >= 0.8 across the 6 doses AND no dose with mean g < 5 gives a section-6 quantum win. Otherwise g is declared not predictive at N = 1,400, and the sentence 'guaranteed to predict about as well for any labels' in READING.md section 4 and FIELD_MAP M2 is reworded to 'within a factor g in an error bound'. P4 (shots): the strongest dose's win is 'shot-robust' if it still passes section 6 at R = 10,000. A dose with no clear difference at any setting is reported as such; no extra doses are added after seeing results.
- **Milestone:** M6 (quantum-kernel inductive bias, estimability under shots) and M2 (validity of the geometric-difference test as this repository uses it); supporting M5 (credibility of the negative benchmark).
- **Kill check:** Before the full run: (1) on seed 0, compute g and dA for all 6 doses on the fraud inputs (~1 min); (2) run the strongest dose (fidelity s0 = 1) on seed 0 with full grids. If the generating QSVM's test accuracy does not exceed the best tuned classical model, surrogates included, by >= 0.05, the construction does not bite on fraud inputs at N = 1,400. Then stop and redesign (e.g. s0 in {1.5, 2} or 3 repeats) before spending the full budget. The synthetic-input scratch pilot already gives +0.20/+0.27 for that dose, so the check is expected to pass. If the IQP-angle surrogate matches the QSVM in that pilot, the 'advantage' is the feature set, and the experiment's purpose narrows to calibrating the section-6 rule.
- **Cost estimate:** Code: new src/qmlrc/engineered.py (~120 lines: out-of-sample label construction, reverse construction, random-label null, IQP-angle surrogate features, alignment). experiments.py: about 50 lines for experiment creditcard_q8_posctl (6 doses + C1-C3 + P4). tests/test_engineered.py (~80 lines: the construction gives s_C = g^2 s_Q on a toy pair of kernels; labels balanced and deterministic; the reverse construction favours the classical kernel). CPU: the scratch pilot took ~35 s per label set per seed (N = 800, reduced grids, one thread). At N = 2,000 with full grids (GradBoost 320 configs ~90 s per seed per READING.md) that is ~2.5 min per label set per seed. 6 doses + C1 + C2 is 8 sets x 5 seeds ~ 100 CPU-min (C3 reuses the twin kernels, ~+10 min; P4 ~+10 min), about 30 min wall time on 4 cores. Split into one experiment per dose/control of ~3-4 min wall time each, within the 10-minute rule.
- **Win teaches:** If P1 and P2 hold, the 5-seed section-6 benchmark can detect a large inductive-bias advantage when one exists, and does not invent one from random labels. The null result on real fraud labels is then a statement about those labels and models, not about a blunt instrument. If P3 also fails, as the pilot suggests, a quantum kernel can win by about 0.2 on suitably aligned labels even when g ~ 3.5 << sqrt(N). The repository must stop citing g alone as the reason no quantum kernel can win, and add a label-aware measure (alignment, s_C(y)/s_Q(y)) to geometry.py. If P4 fails, the engineered advantage lives in a shot-fragile, concentration-onset regime, consistent with M6/M9.
- **Loss teaches:** If P1 fails even at the strongest dose, the benchmark at N = 1,400 with 5 seeds cannot detect an advantage that exists by construction, or tuned trees and MLPs escape the kernel geometry. Either way READING.md's negative results are less informative than stated, and the repository needs a power statement (more seeds or larger N) before claiming 'no quantum model beats classical'. If P2 fails, the pipeline is biased in one direction and every earlier comparison needs re-auditing. If the IQP-angle surrogate ties the QSVM, the edge comes from the feature set, which strengthens K1. If P3 holds, g is confirmed as a usable explanatory criterion at this scale.
- **Closest prior work:** arXiv:2011.01938 (Original engineered-label separation (projected kernel >20% ahead up to 30 qubits; tuned SVM/RF/GBT/AdaBoost/NN baselines).); arXiv:2610.11360 (Same protocol for photonic kernels, with the out-of-sample, reverse and random-label controls adopted here; g found conditioning-sensitive and not tracking the advantage.); arXiv:2407.02553 (Hardware (108-qubit neutral-atom) version with g-based synthetic datasets (abstract).); arXiv:2508.19437 (Relabelling real tabular data to favour a quantum kernel; RBF-only baseline.); arXiv:2610.02636 (g can grow through classical ill-conditioning alone; motivates P3 and the label-aware covariate.); arXiv:2608.29422 (Closed-form Gaussian surrogate of the ZZ/IQP kernel at small bandwidth, used as a classical baseline (abstract).)

**Adversarial review: WEAKENED.** WEAKENED. Shortlisting it as an internal calibration and replication is fine under K7. As written, though, the success rules would give unreliable verdicts in both directions. Already done: the protocol is Huang 2011.01938 App. G with exactly the controls of 2610.11360 (out-of-sample labels, reverse construction, random-label null, alignment covariate). Real-tabular relabelling has been done in 2310.11891 and 2508.19437. P3's question is already answered by 2610.11360, 2610.02636 and 2607.20168 (abstract). Checks I ran in scratch on the real creditcard_q8 inputs (OOS labels, N = 1,400, seeds 0-2): - The pilot's +0.20/+0.27 does not transfer. Fidelity s0 = 1 gave accuracy gaps of +0.043, +0.044, 0.000 and AUPRC gaps of -0.005, +0.052, -0.024. The proposal's own kill check fails on 2 of 3 seeds, and the IQP-angle surrogate tied the QSVM on seed 2. - Only projected s0 = 1 (g of about 13) keeps a consistent but modest edge: about +0.045 accuracy and +0.019 AUPRC. All s0 <= 0.3 doses show none. - The section-6 rule's null false-verdict rate is 8.9% per pair, so P2 ('all 12 pairs no clear difference') fails 53-67% of the time on a fair pipeline. - C1 cannot give a classical win: the tuned QSVM escapes reverse labels through near-Gaussian small-s kernels and ties the RBF-SVM (0.890 vs 0.890). Specification errors: - s0 = 0.3 is not in the fidelity grid. - The 'I + pi^2 Q' surrogate is the wrong formula for the repo's circuit; there it is exp(-s^2 |dx|^2 / 4), i.e. just an RBF. - The kill check uses accuracy while P1 uses AUPRC. - P3 cannot settle a bound-based sentence that should simply be reworded from Huang's inequality. On these inputs P3 would probably 'pass' and wrongly keep that sentence. Fixes: re-pilot on the real inputs; use one metric; fix the doses; calibrate P2; redesign C1; restrict P1 to the fraud-benchmark models; add an oracle arm; drop P3; state the power analytically (80%-power MDE of about 0.017 AUPRC for the kernels). Sources read: - full text: 2011.01938, 2610.11360, 2610.02636, 2508.19437, 2608.29422, 2310.11891; - abstract only: 2407.02553, 2211.16551, 2503.05602, 2607.20168, 2610.09717. Scratch files (no repository file was touched) are in /tmp/claude-0/-home-user-quantum-ml-reality-check/3242e8f8-b193-546c-b381-3fe888e7979d/scratchpad/round01/t6_review: - rule_oc.py; - posctl_fraud.py and posctl_proj.py, with logs posctl_fraud_seed0.log, posctl_proj_seed0.log and strong_*_seed{1,2}.log; - c1_check.py, with log c1_seed0.log; - smalls.py.

- *Already done:* Yes, almost exactly. The protocol is already published; only its application here (IQP kernels on fraud inputs, tuned GBT/MLP, the section-6 rule) is new, so under K7 it can only be shortlisted as a replication / internal calibration. (1) Huang et al. 2011.01938 (full text, pp. 1-3, 5, 8, 23-24, 31-32): - the engineered labels: y' = sqrt(K_Q) v from the top eigenvector of eq. G3/L14, then sign or median binarisation with 10% random labels (eq. L15); - grid-searched Gaussian SVM, linear SVM, RF, GBT, AdaBoost and NN baselines, "robust across tested classical methods". (2) Kar & Babu H 2610.11360 (full text) already ran this exact control set, which the proposal lifts: - out-of-sample labels through the kernel expansion; - reverse construction (-0.048); - random-label null (+0.009); - the alignment gap dA as a covariate; - pre-registered "designed OOS task as positive control" and "random labels as null control" (H3/H4). They also showed g is conditioning-sensitive and dissociated from the learnable advantage (g fell 5.6x while the advantage stayed unchanged). Their baselines were kernel ridge and RFF only. (3) Relabelling real tabular inputs with Huang's construction was already done: - Egginger et al. 2310.11891 (full text): 11 real datasets, 6 qubits, kernel-SVM baselines. Separation 0.15-0.16 appeared only at GD of about 1000-2600, and about 0 at GD 3.5-8.3. They also flag GD outliers from near-singular classical Gram matrices. - Sakhnenko et al. 2508.19437 (full text): 8 PCA features, RBF-only, plus the g-saturation construction in their Appendix A. (4) The P3 question (does g predict the gap?) has already been answered negatively: - 2610.11360 (above); - Friedewald et al. 2610.02636 (full text): g tracks 1/sqrt(lambda_min(K_C)) with r = 0.991, and they add a label-permutation control; - Shen 2607.20168 (abstract only): g >> 1 but does not predict out-of-sample gains, rho = -0.20. What remains new is only internal: whether THIS repo's 5-seed section-6 pipeline, with its tuned trees/MLP, registers a built-in effect on the fraud inputs. That is a legitimate replication, but it adds nothing to the literature, and P3 adds nothing at all.
- *Classical can match:* For a calibration this angle bites in three places. (a) The IQP-angle surrogate (RBF on cos/sin of s*x_i and s^2*x_i*x_j) largely erases the fidelity-kernel edge. - In my scratch run on the real creditcard_q8 inputs (OOS labels, N = 1,400, seeds 0-2, reduced grids, CV on accuracy), it was the best classical model on accuracy in most doses. - At fidelity s0 = 1 it tied the QSVM on seed 2 (0.858 vs 0.858). - Across seeds the fidelity s0 = 1 gap against the best classical model was +0.043, +0.044, 0.000 in accuracy and -0.005, +0.052, -0.024 in AUPRC (the RBF-SVM beat the QSVM on AUPRC on seeds 0 and 2). - So for the fidelity doses the "advantage" is mostly the feature set (K1). - Only the projected s0 = 1 dose (g = 12.4-13.2) kept a consistent edge over every classical model: +0.054, +0.037, +0.045 accuracy and +0.024, +0.020, +0.012 AUPRC. (b) Baseline (ii) is not what the proposal says. - For the repo's 2-repeat unshifted PennyLane IQPEmbedding, the small-bandwidth kernel is exp(-s^2 |dx|^2 / 4). I checked this numerically: (1-K)/(s^2|u|^2) = 0.250 for s <= 0.1. - Its maximum deviation from that Gaussian is 0.008 at s = 0.1, 0.13 at s = 0.2 and 0.44 at s = 0.3. - So it is just an RBF-SVM with a wider gamma grid. It is redundant with RBF-SVM/LogReg and irrelevant at the s0 = 1 doses. (c) The reverse direction: the tuned QSVM grid contains near-Gaussian kernels (s = 0.02-0.05). It therefore "classically matches" RBF-generated labels, so C1 cannot show a classical win (see access_model_issue). Exact classical simulation of the 8-qubit kernel erases any gap; the proposal already concedes this.
- *Access model issue:* Classical data only. Both sides get the same 1,400 labelled pairs and the same folds, so the fairness is acceptable and no physical access asymmetry exists. The asymmetries are in the hypothesis classes and the control design, not in physical access. (1) Oracle prior. The label generator lies inside the QSVM's hypothesis class, and its s0 is in the grid. The proposal admits this. It is fine for a calibration but must never be read as advantage. (2) The grids nest one way only, which breaks control C1. - The quantum bandwidth grid contains Gaussian look-alikes at small s. - The classical grid contains nothing resembling K_Q(s = 1). - Measured on seed 0 of the fraud inputs: reverse labels generated by the RBF kernel (f = 1 or 0.3) and made adversarial to K_Q(s = 1) are hard for the QSVM fixed at s = 1 (accuracy 0.715 / 0.702). - But the QSVM tuned over its grid picks s = 0.02 / 0.05 and ties the RBF-SVM (0.890 vs 0.890; 0.878 vs 0.888). - So P2's requirement that "the tuned RBF-SVM beats the generating QSVM" in C1 fails for a structural reason, not because of pipeline bias. - Fixing the QSVM at s0 in C1 would instead handicap the quantum side relative to P1, where it is tuned. - The geometry.py g_tra < 0.045 rule excludes exactly those small-s quantum kernels the tuned SVM escapes to. The g calculation therefore does not model the regularised learner. - The C1 "tuned RBF kernel" is undefined (tuned on which labels?). (3) Wrong benchmark calibrated. P1 includes two surrogates that the fraud benchmark never contained, so P1 does not calibrate the pipeline it claims to calibrate.
- *Feasibility:* Runnable at 8 qubits with exact simulation. - Kernels for 2,000 points take under 1 minute. The OOS construction (eigenproblems on 1,400 x 1,400 matrices) takes seconds. - The cost estimate is optimistic. On balanced labels with 10% noise, MLP (max_iter = 2000) and GBT fits are much slower than on the fraud labels. - My measurements were made on a shared 4-core machine while another agent's process was using about 2.5 cores, so they overstate the cost somewhat. Rough extrapolation to the full repo grids: 6-10 CPU-min per label set per seed (the proposal says about 2.5). - That puts each 5-seed dose experiment at roughly 8-12 wall-min on 4 cores, at or above the 10-minute rule. Split by seed or model, or cap the MLP iterations. Decisiveness is the real problem. - The pilot's +0.20/+0.27 came from in-sample labels on Gaussian inputs at 560 training points. It does not transfer. - On the real inputs with OOS labels, effects are about +0.045 accuracy / +0.02 AUPRC at projected s0 = 1, and about 0 for every s0 <= 0.3 dose (accuracy -0.004 and 0.000). - The proposal's own kill check (fidelity s0 = 1, accuracy gap >= 0.05) fails on seeds 0 and 2. - P2 fails about half the time on a fair pipeline: the per-pair two-sided false-verdict rate of the section-6 rule is 8.9%, so 12 pairs give 53-67% (simulated, 400k draws). - C1 cannot produce a classical win. - P3 is statistically meaningless with 6 confounded doses (the one-sided critical Spearman rho at n = 6 is about 0.83). On the fraud inputs it will likely "pass", because the only dose with an edge has the largest g, which would wrongly license the 'for any labels' sentence. So the outcome as specified would not decide the hypothesis reliably in either direction.
- *Technical errors:* The fidelity bandwidth grid (src/qmlrc/models.py FIDELITY_SCALES = [0.02, 0.05, 0.1, 0.2, 0.5, 1.0]) does not contain 0.3. 'Both bandwidth grids contain the generating s0' is false for the fidelity s0 = 0.3 dose: in my run the QSVM selected s = 0.2.; Baseline (ii) is mis-specified. M = I + pi^2 Q is 2608.29422's result for ONE layer under the pi-shifted (Qiskit) convention. That paper gives L >= 2 only numerically, and under the unshifted convention M = I. For the repo's 2-repeat PennyLane IQPEmbedding the leading term is exp(-s^2 |dx|^2 / 4), verified numerically, valid only for s <~ 0.1. It is an RBF kernel with gamma = s^2/4, not a 'parameter-free' new baseline.; P2 specificity is mis-calibrated. Under the null the section-6 rule declares 'beats' 4.5% of the time in each direction (8.9% two-sided per pair). With 2 QSVMs x 6 classical models = 12 pairs, 'every pair no clear difference' in C2 fails with probability about 0.53 (shared noise, rho = 0.5) to 0.67 (independent). A fair pipeline would then be declared biased, with 'every earlier comparison needs re-auditing'.; C1 is structurally unable to produce the required classical win. Seed 0 of the fraud inputs: the tuned QSVM escapes reverse labels via s = 0.02-0.05 and ties the RBF-SVM (0.890 vs 0.890; 0.878 vs 0.888), while the QSVM fixed at s = 1 scores 0.715.; The pilot effect size does not transfer to the stated protocol. On the real creditcard_q8 inputs with OOS labels at N = 1,400 (3 seeds), fidelity s0 = 1 gives accuracy gaps of +0.043 / +0.044 / 0.000 and AUPRC gaps of -0.005 / +0.052 / -0.024 against the best classical model, not +0.20. The kill check fails on 2 of 3 seeds. The strongest dose is projected s0 = 1, not fidelity s0 = 1.; Metric inconsistency. The kill check uses accuracy (>= 0.05) while P1 is decided on AUPRC. AUPRC gaps were 2-3x smaller and sometimes reversed (fidelity s0 = 1, seed 0: +0.043 accuracy but -0.005 AUPRC against the RBF-SVM).; P3 is ill-posed. The rewording 'within a factor g in an error bound' follows from s_C <= g^2 s_Q alone, as Huang states on p. 5. READING.md's sentence faithfully paraphrases Huang's own p. 2 wording ('guaranteed to provide similar or better performance ... independent of the function values or labels'). A Spearman threshold of 0.8 across 6 doses that differ in both kernel family and bandwidth is underpowered and confounded, and neither outcome can validate or refute a worst-case bound.; loss_teaches has a non-sequitur. If tuned trees or MLPs 'escape' the kernel geometry on the quantum kernel's own engineered labels, that STRENGTHENS the negative fraud result rather than weakening it. A P1 failure may also simply mean the OOS construction yields small effects on heavy-tailed PCA inputs. There is no oracle arm to tell these apart.; Mechanism wording: s_Q = 1 for the raw y' is tiny relative to N. The 's_Q/N not small' argument holds only after rescaling the binarised labels to unit scale (s_Q' = N / v^T K_Q v). The label normalisation must be stated.; The note on 2407.02553 over-reads the abstract. The abstract does not say that the g-based synthetic-dataset comparison was done at 108 qubits or on hardware.
- *Required fixes:* Re-pilot on the actual creditcard_q8 inputs with the OOS construction (not Gaussian inputs, not in-sample labels) before pre-registering. Make projected s0 = 1 the designated strongest dose. Expect about +0.02 AUPRC / +0.045 accuracy, and delete the '+0.2 at g ~ 3.5' expectation from win_teaches.; Use one metric throughout: AUPRC in the kill check and in P1, with the CV selection metric stated. Alternatively pre-register accuracy as primary for balanced engineered labels, with AUPRC secondary.; Fix the dose list. Fidelity s0 must lie in FIDELITY_SCALES (e.g. {0.1, 0.5, 1.0}); otherwise label s0 = 0.3 explicitly as a mismatched-grid dose.; Replace P2's 'every pair no clear difference' with a calibrated false-positive test. Either run several random-label draws and compare the count of section-6 verdicts with the nominal 4.5% per direction (binomial 95% bound), or pre-register the expected about 1 spurious verdict in 12 pairs and fail only above the 95% quantile.; Redesign C1 so a classical win is achievable. Options: (a) a classical-native target, such as axis-aligned threshold or shallow-tree labels, where GBT is expected to beat every kernel; or (b) reverse labels made adversarial to the whole QSVM grid, verified on seed 0 to make the tuned QSVM lose before pre-registration; or (c) a symmetric comparison at fixed generating hyperparameters in both P1 and C1. Define the RBF generator precisely.; Restrict P1 to the models of the fraud benchmark being calibrated (LogReg, RBF-SVM, GBT, MLP vs QSVMs). Report the IQP-angle surrogate as a separate K1 question (feature set vs interference).; Drop baseline (ii), or describe it correctly as exp(-s^2 |dx|^2 / 4), an RBF with a wider gamma grid valid for s <~ 0.1.; Add an oracle arm: the QSVM at the generating kernel with tuned C, compared with Bayes accuracy 0.90. This separates 'the construction did not bite' from 'the benchmark could not detect'. Rewrite loss_teaches accordingly: a classical escape strengthens the null result.; Drop P3 as an experimental decision rule. Reword READING.md section 4 and FIELD_MAP M2 now, from the inequality: the classical error bound is within a factor g of the quantum bound, so moderate gaps are possible whenever g^2 s_Q >~ N at unit label scale. Cite Huang p. 5. Keep g, dA and s_C(y)/s_Q(y) on the binarised labels with a label-permutation null (as in 2610.02636) as descriptive covariates only.; State the power analytically now. From the paired AUPRC SDs in RESULTS.md (about 0.011 for the kernels, 0.022-0.025 for the VQC) and the rule's power curve (55% at 1 sigma, 84% at 1.5 sigma, 97% at 2 sigma), the 80%-power MDE is about 0.017 AUPRC (kernels) and 0.035 (VQC). The positive control is then informative only as a check at a realistic effect size (about 0.02), which the projected s0 = 1 dose provides.; Re-budget compute: per dose experiment about 30-50 CPU-min on noisy balanced labels. Split by seed or model, or cap MLP iterations / use early stopping, to respect the 10-minute rule.; Label the card 'replication of a known protocol (Huang 2011.01938 App. G; controls of 2610.11360; real-tabular relabelling as in 2310.11891 and 2508.19437)'.
- *Further prior work:* arXiv:2310.11891 (Egginger, Sakhnenko & Lorenz, A Hyperparameter Study for Quantum Kernel Methods (full text read). Huang-style relabelling of 11 real tabular datasets at 6 qubits, with kernel-SVM baselines only. Separation 0.15-0.16 only at GD of about 1000-2600; none at GD 3.5-8.3. GD outliers come from near-singular classical Gram matrices. GD and accuracy depend on bandwidth in opposite ways.); arXiv:2607.20168 (Shen, Quantum Kernels and the Cross-Section of Stock Returns (abstract only). g >> 1 throughout, yet it does not predict out-of-sample gains (rho = -0.20). Proposes kernel-swap controls. Bears directly on P3.); arXiv:2503.05602 (Florez-Ablan, Roth & Schnabel, On the similarity of bandwidth-tuned quantum kernels and classical kernels (abstract only). Optimally bandwidth-tuned quantum kernels resemble RBF or polynomial kernels. This explains why the tuned QSVM escapes the C1 reverse labels.); arXiv:2211.16551 (Slattery, Shaydulin et al., Numerical evidence against advantage with quantum fidelity kernels on classical data (abstract only). Hyperparameter tuning makes quantum kernels well approximated by classical kernels.); arXiv:2610.09717 (Micah et al., Pareto-optimal quantum kernel selection ... malware beaconing (abstract only). A fidelity kernel with small g beat the tuned baseline, while a projected kernel with g ~ 89 > sqrt(N) gained only marginally. g is neither necessary nor sufficient.)
