## T7. Learning quantum phases and classifying quantum-state data

### I-20261012-12: SU(2)-symmetric variational models for many-body dynamics

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | external batch B |
| Hypothesis | A symmetry-constrained variational quantum model outperforms classical neural quantum states (RNN/RBM) at predicting long-time dynamics of SU(2)-symmetric spin chains. |
| Mechanism | Enforcing symmetry restricts the search to valid manifolds, avoiding the overhead classical NQS face. |
| Data | Quantum (simulated Heisenberg chain measurement data). |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | 6-qubit Heisenberg chain; symmetric ansatz vs tuned RNN/RBM; metric: fidelity of predicted evolution; success: higher fidelity with fewer parameters. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | Classical equivariant models may match. |
| References cited | 2010.02174, 2405.02027 |
| Reference checks | 2010.02174: **CLAIM NOT IN PAPER** (Exists (Liu, Arunachalam, Temme). It is a discrete-log classification task with a fault-tolerant quantum kernel and says nothing about symmetric variational dynamics or NQS. Abstract read.); 2405.02027: **CLAIM NOT IN PAPER** (Exists (Molteni, Gyurik, Dunjko). It covers learning observables from measured-out data under BQP-hardness assumptions, with nothing about SU(2)-symmetric ansatze against NQS for dynamics. Abstract read.) |
| Technical problems | With H known, 'predicting long-time dynamics' is simulation, not learning. At 6 qubits exact evolution is trivial classically, so the relevant classical baseline is exact diagonalisation, not an RNN or RBM.; A quantum device that knows H just runs Trotterised evolution; a variational symmetric model adds nothing for a known Hamiltonian.; 'Higher fidelity with fewer parameters' is not an advantage criterion: parameter count is not a resource that classical exact simulation lacks.; The mechanism is false as stated. Classical NQS can be restricted to symmetric subspaces (2407.20065), so 'overhead classical NQS face' is not intrinsic.; 'Measurement data' is undefined. If the task is learning dynamics or a Hamiltonian from data, that is a different task and the card neither specifies nor bounds it. |
| Kill-criteria check | K6, K4, K7 |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2512.23009 (Symmetry-preserving variational circuits for the Heisenberg chain (ground state) beat a hardware-efficient ansatz; benchmarked against exact diagonalisation.); arXiv:2210.08566 (General equivariant-QNN theory, including SU(2)-equivariant QCNNs.); arXiv:2407.20065 (Classical NQS confined to symmetric subspaces: classical models can encode the same symmetry.) |
| Status | **REJECTED** |
| Decision note | Not a learning test. Predicting the dynamics of a known 6-qubit Heisenberg chain is a 64-dimensional exact classical simulation, so tuned NQS is a strawman baseline (K4). There is no well-posed quantum-data learning task at <=10 qubits (K6). Symmetry-preserving variational simulation and SU(2)-equivariant QNNs already exist, and classical NQS can impose symmetry too (K7). |

### I-20261012-22: Symmetry-preserving quantum classification of topological phases

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | external batch C |
| Hypothesis | A symmetry-equivariant quantum classifier distinguishes SPT and trivial chain states near the boundary with fewer copies than classical shadows, invariant GNNs and measurement-data classifiers. |
| Mechanism | Symmetry-preserving ansatz plus access to copies measures correlations without reconstructing the state; claimed advantage is statistical efficiency. |
| Data | Quantum ground states of 6-8-site chains with symmetry-preserving perturbations. |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | Exact diagonalization; vs shadows, invariant GNN, reconstruction-and-classify at equal copy budget; metric: copies to reach 90% on held-out couplings; success: reproducible reduction in copies. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | String-order parameters may suffice; MPS methods may match. |
| References cited | 2205.06217, 2306.16028 |
| Reference checks | 2205.06217: **OK** (Exists (Meyer et al.). Supports symmetric or equivariant ansatze as inductive bias (better generalisation on toy problems). It makes no copy-efficiency claim against classical shadows for SPT data. Abstract read.); 2306.16028: **CLAIM NOT IN PAPER** (Exists (Gyurik & Dunjko). It discusses phases of matter and the limits of classical shadows in learning-theory terms, but its separations are computational (hardness assumptions), not reductions in copy count for symmetric classifiers. Abstract only.) |
| Technical problems | No access model is given. A single-copy quantum classifier has at most a constant copy advantage at n<=10 (QCNN depth 1); a multi-copy one needs >=2n qubits of quantum memory, which must be charged.; At 6-8 sites the finite-size crossover is broad. Labels taken from the thermodynamic-limit diagram are noisy exactly 'near the boundary', where the metric is evaluated (noted in 2408.12739).; With a fixed Hamiltonian family and symmetric perturbations the data stay locally easy. Scratch pilot at n=10: exact <=3-local Pauli features give 0.96 CV accuracy, and the exact SOP Z1X2X4X6Z7 averages 0.90 (SPT) against 0.06 (trivial).; 'Invariant GNN' baseline: its input (shadows? exact expectations?) is undefined. |
| Kill-criteria check | K7, K4 |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2606.28199 (HNN needs about 10x fewer shots than a classical NN on Pauli-6 data for the surface-code phase (N=9-25); the classical baseline is linear in rho only.); arXiv:2408.12739 (Standard SPT and phase datasets are locally easy; a shadow-based classical surrogate matches QCNNs.); arXiv:2210.08566 (SU(2)-equivariant QCNN for bond-alternating Heisenberg phases.) |
| Status | **MERGED into I-20261012-35** |
| Decision note | Same mechanism as 35 and 28: a symmetry-respecting coherent classifier against shadow and measurement-data classifiers at equal copies. Its 'topological phase' angle is kept in the best version as dataset B (surface code, closest prior 2606.28199). |

### I-20261012-28: Symmetry-preserving QCNN for quantum phase classification

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | external batch D |
| Hypothesis | A symmetry-preserving QCNN beats tuned classical learners at phase classification from finite-size quantum states under symmetry-preserving perturbations. |
| Mechanism | Coherent, symmetry-respecting measurement of non-local correlations; whether it helps beyond classical access to the same copies is the question. |
| Data | Quantum: copies of cluster-Ising ground states. |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | 8-10 qubits exact; QCNN vs RBF-SVM, GBT, MLP and symmetry-aware classical models on matched measurement/shadow data; balanced accuracy on held-out perturbations vs copy budget. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | Finite chain may not express the phase; string-order features may match. |
| References cited | reference unknown |
| Reference checks | reference unknown: **NOT VERIFIED** (No reference given. The QCNN paper is arXiv:1810.03787 (Cong, Choi, Lukin), resolved through the arXiv API and partly read.) |
| Technical problems | At n<=10 a QCNN with 3-to-1 pooling has at most one pooling layer (n=9). Cong et al.'s advantage, which grows with depth (ratio about 1.73 e^{0.28d}, fitted at N=135), cannot show beyond d=1.; 'Balanced accuracy vs copy budget' must also fix the training copies. Parameter-shift training of a QCNN uses thousands of shots per loss evaluation per state (2408.12739 quotes 5000-10000), far more than shadow learners use.; The unscrambled cluster-Ising data are locally easy (see 22). The symmetry-preserving perturbations the card proposes do not remove this (scratch pilot). |
| Kill-criteria check | K7 |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:1810.03787 (Exact QCNN, copy advantage over single SOPs near the boundary.); arXiv:2109.05909 (7-qubit hardware QCNN beats direct measurement in fidelity.); arXiv:2504.04101 (Local partial-tomography Neyman-Pearson test beats QCNN and exact QCNN in copies (L=15-81).) |
| Status | **MERGED into I-20261012-35** |
| Decision note | Duplicate of 35: a symmetry-preserving QCNN against tuned classical learners on matched measurement or shadow data, cluster-Ising, 8-10 qubits. |

### I-20261012-30: Task-aligned projected kernels on quantum-state data under shot limits

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | external batch D |
| Hypothesis | A task-aligned projected quantum kernel beats full fidelity kernels and classical-shadow RBF models on shot-limited classification of quantum states. |
| Mechanism | Projection onto useful observables avoids the concentration and shot cost of fidelity kernels; the advantage sought is in state-copy efficiency. |
| Data | Quantum ground states near a small-chain phase boundary. |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | 6-10 qubits; projected vs fidelity kernels at 100-10,000 shots per entry; vs RBF-SVM, GBT, MLP on classical shadows from the same number of copies; balanced accuracy vs total preparations. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | Projected observables may be easy for shadows; no asymptotic law at small n. |
| References cited | 2208.11060, 2111.05451 |
| Reference checks | 2208.11060: **OK** (Exists (Thanasilp, Wang, Cerezo, Holmes). Concentration from global measurements and entanglement, and the resulting shot cost, are as the card uses them. Abstract read; FIELD_MAP has full text.); 2111.05451: **CLAIM NOT IN PAPER** (Exists, and is by Shaydulin & Wild (not Canatar et al.). It concerns the bandwidth of embeddings of classical data. Quantum-state inputs have no embedding bandwidth, so it applies here only by analogy, to gamma of the projected kernel.) |
| Technical problems | On quantum-state inputs a projected kernel is a function of k-RDMs, which single-qubit randomized measurements estimate directly. 'Projected quantum kernel vs classical-shadow RBF' therefore compares two classical learners.; The fidelity kernel between two different training states needs both states at once (SWAP test, coherent access across experiments) and O(N^2 R) copies in total. A test state alone needs N_train*R copies, so 'total preparations' accounting kills it at any realistic budget.; Fidelity kernels between ground states on opposite sides of a boundary concentrate. 2603.18211 finds shot costs scaling as k_repr^-2, and our own repository found the fidelity kernel the most shot-fragile (READING.md).; 'Task-aligned' choice of observables is classical feature selection. |
| Kill-criteria check | K5, K7 |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2603.18211 (Fidelity-kernel SVM on spin-chain ground states; SWAP-test shot bounds grow with symmetry.); arXiv:2602.23501 (Sample-optimal coherent overlap measurement for classifying quantum data; local strategies argued to be exponential.); arXiv:2106.12627 (Shadow kernel on classical shadows for phase classification.) |
| Status | **MERGED into I-20261012-38** |
| Decision note | Its only quantum-vs-classical axis is the fidelity kernel against shadow kernels at equal copies, which is 38's question. The 'task-aligned projected kernel' on state inputs is itself a measure-first classical learner. |

### I-20261012-35: Fair test of QCNN phase recognition against classical-shadow machine learning

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | Claude |
| Hypothesis | On symmetry-protected phase recognition (cluster-Ising chain, n=8-10), a QCNN with coherent access is matched by tuned classical ML on classical-shadow features at the same copy budget. |
| Mechanism | Tests the QCNN claim against Huang et al.'s theorem that classical ML on shadows learns phases efficiently; expected outcome is no advantage except perhaps at tiny copy budgets. |
| Data | Quantum ground states from exact diagonalization. |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | Copies per state in {10, 100, 1000}; QCNN vs shadow features + RBF-SVM, GBT, MLP and a string-order logistic regression; balanced accuracy vs copies on held-out couplings; success (quantum): better at equal copies. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | Shadows near-optimal by theory: likely a killing experiment. |
| References cited | 2106.12627 (Huang et al., provably efficient ML for quantum many-body problems), QCNN paper: ID to verify |
| Reference checks | 2106.12627 (Huang et al., provably efficient ML for quantum many-body problems): **OK** (Exists, authors match. Thm 2 / Cor. 1: shadow-kernel ML classifies phases with polynomial data if a well-conditioned function of few-body RDMs separates them. This is conditional, not a theorem for all SPT phases; costs grow as 12^r and n^{r d_p}. Relevant pages read.); QCNN paper: ID to verify: **OK** (Resolved to arXiv:1810.03787, Cong, Choi, Lukin, 'Quantum Convolutional Neural Networks' (arXiv API; pages 1-4, 6-8, 10 read).) |
| Technical problems | The dataset is locally easy (scratch pilot at n=10: <=3-local exact features give 0.96 CV accuracy, one feature has \|corr\|=0.98, the SOP separates 0.90 from 0.06). The expected 'no advantage' result is close to predetermined.; Only test copies are budgeted. QCNN training shots (thousands per loss evaluation per state) must be counted, or exact-expectation training declared as a favour to the quantum side.; Labels taken from the thermodynamic phase diagram are wrong near the boundary at n<=10. Training on the solvable line and excluding a window are needed.; Huang et al.'s theorem is conditional (Assumption 1). At small n it does not by itself guarantee that shadows win at a fixed small copy budget. |
| Kill-criteria check | K7, K4, K5 |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2408.12739 (Shadow-based classical surrogate matches or beats QCNNs on cluster, Haldane, XXX and ANNNI phase datasets; datasets are locally easy.); arXiv:2504.04101 (Local partial-tomography NP test beats QCNN and exact QCNN at equal copies on cluster-Ising (L=15-81).); arXiv:2307.03711 (Claims an exponential copy reduction of the constant-depth QCNN over local Pauli estimation of the multiscale SOP.); arXiv:1810.03787 (Original claim of a copy advantage over single SOPs.); arXiv:2109.05909 (7-qubit hardware QCNN recognises the SPT phase better than direct SOP measurement.) |
| Status | **REJECTED (shortlisted at triage, killed in adversarial review)** |
| Decision note | As written it largely repeats 2408.12739 and 2504.04101 on a locally easy dataset (K7 risk). It survives because a copy-matched test of the published QCNN and HNN copy-efficiency claims, with tuned grids and the paired §6 rule, has not been done. K4 is avoided by tuned grids on both sides and K5 by charging every copy (training and test). Becomes the best version below. |
| Decisive test | Cluster-Ising (Cong et al. Hamiltonian), n=9. Train on the solvable line h2=0, test on the line h1=0.5J excluding \|h2-0.423\|<0.1. Compare the exact d=1 QCNN and a trained QCNN with tuned classical learners on Pauli-6 and product-basis data: SOP threshold, multi-basis SOP/stabilizer logistic regression, LogReg/RBF-SVM/GBT/MLP on shadow features, shadow-kernel SVM. Equal copies per test state S in {1,4,16,64,256}, equal training copies. Primary metric: balanced accuracy at S=16, 5 seeds, §6 rule. Pre-check: Cong's exact sample complexity M_min, QCNN against the single SOP, must give a ratio >=1.5 or the claim does not exist at this size. |

### I-20261012-38: Geometric difference on quantum-state inputs: is there room for an advantage?

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Proposed by | Claude |
| Hypothesis | When inputs are quantum states and classical kernels act on classical-shadow features of the same states, the geometric difference g can exceed sqrt(N), unlike our classical-data result. |
| Mechanism | Applies Huang et al.'s criterion to quantum data, with our geometry module, to measure room for advantage before training any model. |
| Data | Quantum ground states across a phase diagram. |
| Minimal test (as proposed) | n=8-10 TFIM/cluster states, N=200; g between quantum kernels on states and classical kernels on shadow features (Pauli expectations up to weight 2, finite copies); success: identify regimes with g >= sqrt(N), then test learning there. |
| Main risk (as proposed) | Shadow features may capture everything (small g). |
| References cited | 2011.01938, 2106.12627 |
| Reference checks | 2011.01938: **OK** (Exists. Geometric difference and the g >= sqrt(N) criterion are as cited. In the paper the criterion is necessary, not sufficient, and needs the label-dependent check. Defined for classical inputs; applying it to state inputs is the card's own extension.); 2106.12627: **OK** (Exists. The shadow kernel definition (eq. 6) and its O(n T^2) cost were read.) |
| Technical problems | Pauli features of weight <=2 handicap the classical side for SPT data (string order is non-local), so a large g would be by construction. The all-body shadow kernel with tuned gamma and tau must be included.; Precise statement, identity checked numerically in scratch for n=3: as T goes to infinity the inner sum of the shadow kernel equals sum_A a^{n-\|A\|} b^{\|A\|} tr(rho_A rho'_A), with a, b > 0 set by gamma/n. Hence K_shadow is PSD-larger than tau b^n K_fid. With trace normalisation and tau=(a+b)^-n this gives g(K_shadow \|\| K_fid) <= sqrt(e) ((a+b)/b)^{n/2}, about 53 at n=10 for large gamma. That bound is constant at fixed n but does not exclude g >= sqrt(200)=14, so g must be measured.; Comparing exact quantum kernels with finite-copy classical kernels is unfair. The fidelity kernel needs coherent access to two different states and O(N^2 R) copies.; g >= sqrt(N) is necessary, not sufficient. Also, fidelity kernels across a phase boundary concentrate (2603.18211). |
| Kill-criteria check | K5, K7 |
| Closest prior work (verified) | arXiv:2011.01938 (Geometric-difference methodology (classical inputs).); arXiv:2603.18211 (Shot cost of fidelity kernels on ground states.); arXiv:2602.23501 (Coherent overlap kernels on quantum data; argues local strategies are exponential.) |
| Status | **SHORTLISTED (low priority; not part of the theme's reviewed best version)** |
| Decision note | A cheap, well-defined diagnostic (reuses qmlrc.geometry) and no prior application to quantum-state inputs was found. It must compare kernels at matched copies (K5), and the all-body shadow kernel with tuned gamma must be in the classical family: restricting to weight-2 Paulis would be a handicap. Low priority: g measures room for advantage, not advantage. |
| Decisive test | Ground states of cluster-Ising at n=8 and 10, N=200. Compute the regularised g (Huang Eqs. F19, L8-L11, as in qmlrc.geometry) of (a) the fidelity kernel from R SWAP-test shots per pair against (b) the best classical kernel: shadow kernel (2106.12627 eq. 6) over a gamma x tau grid, and RBF on shadow features. Use matched per-state copy budgets in {64, 256, 1024}, and include the exact infinite-copy kernels. Room exists only if g >= sqrt(N) on >=4 of 5 seeds at some budget, followed by the label-dependent check. |

### T7 best version: Copy-matched phase recognition at <=10 qubits: QCNN / hybrid coherent-readout networks vs tuned multi-shot classical-shadow learners

*Kind:* killing experiment. *Built from:* I-20261012-35, I-20261012-22, I-20261012-28, I-20261012-38, I-20261012-30.

- **Hypothesis:** Two published claims of a copy advantage fit within 10 qubits: (A) the Z2xZ2 SPT phase of the cluster-Ising chain (n=9; exact and trained QCNN, 1810.03787) and (B) the 3x3 surface code in a field against random Pauli product states (N=9; hybrid circuit+NN, 2606.28199). On both, the claim under test is that a learner with a single-copy entangling measurement needs fewer test copies than the best tuned classical learner on product/randomized Pauli data for the same balanced accuracy. Pre-registered expectation: no clear difference in A (data are locally easy). In B, the published ~7-10x gap is reproduced against the paper's linear single-shot Pauli-6 network but largely disappears against nonlinear multi-shot classical learners.
- **Mechanism:** Claim side: the QCNN measures, in one shot, a multiscale string order parameter (a sum of products of many SOPs). Its distribution is sharper near criticality, and estimating it with product bases needs exponentially many bases at maximal depth (2307.03711). In B, a shallow circuit initialised as the inverse preparation circuit maps the topological state close to a fixed bitstring, which maximises single-shot distinguishability. Counter side: at n<=10 the QCNN depth is 1 (constant factor at best), and the SPT data are locally easy (2408.12739; our pilot: 0.96 CV accuracy from exact <=3-local features). In B the trivial class is product Pauli eigenstates, which have pure single-qubit marginals while the surface code has maximally mixed ones. A classical learner allowed nonlinear multi-shot statistics (same-basis collision rates, shadow kernel) sees this, whereas the published baseline used only functions linear in rho. Back-of-envelope (independent pairs): a non-learned collision test reaches about 91% at S=7, 99.5% at S=13 and 99.997% at S=20 shots, against the published N=9 budgets of S_inf=30 (HNN) and 200 (Pauli-6 NN).
- **Data:** Quantum data, ground states from exact diagonalisation, no files. (A) H=-J sum Z_iX_{i+1}Z_{i+2} - h1 sum X_i - h2 sum X_iX_{i+1}, open chain n=9 (and n=10 for the constant-depth QCNN), restricted to the symmetric sector P_odd=P_even=+1. Training: 40 states on the exactly solvable line h2=0, h1 in [0,2] (transition at h1=J). Test: 60 states on h1=0.5J, h2 in [-0.6,1.0], excluding |h2-0.423|<0.1 (the iDMRG critical point quoted in 1810.03787) plus a finite-size window fixed beforehand from the exact SOP curve. (B) Rotated 3x3 surface code (9 qubits) in a field, Hamiltonian exactly as in 2606.28199 Sec. IV (not transcribed here; must be copied from the paper before coding). Topological class: ground states for h in [0,0.25]. Trivial class: random tensor products of Pauli eigenstates (1-design). 80 training and 200 test states per seed. Seeds 0-4 redraw couplings, product states, measurement randomness and initialisations.
- **Quantum model:** (A) The exact Cong et al. QCNN (Fig. 2b: one 3-to-1 convolution-pooling unit, then a fully connected layer measuring ZXZ) at n=9, and its constant-depth form (CZ disentangler, X measurements, classical Boolean post-processing; 2307.03711) at n=10. Also a trainable translation-invariant QCNN (2-3-qubit gates, <=2 convolution-pooling units) in PennyLane+JAX. (B) The HNN of 2606.28199: Ry and CZ(phi) layers initialised at the inverse preparation circuit, plus a feed-forward NN on the 9-bit outcomes. Trained with exact outcome distributions and Adam rather than evolution strategies, which favours the quantum side. Inference averages the single-shot scores over S shots, and for fairness may also use set-level features of the S bitstrings (e.g. collision counts). Quantum grids: <=16 configurations per model (depth/layers x learning rate x readout width).
- **Classical baselines:** All tuned by grid search on the same grouped CV folds of the training states, with the selection metric balanced accuracy, and never on a smaller grid than the quantum model they are compared with. (A) (1) SOP threshold: copies measured in the single product basis Z X 1 X ... Z, length L in {5,7} chosen by CV (Cong's baseline). (2) Designed multi-basis learner: copies split over the 2-3 product bases that give all SOP lengths and the ZXZ stabilisers, then logistic regression. (3) Pauli-6 classical shadows, with features = all <=3-local Pauli expectations in 3-site windows plus estimated SOPs of every length, fed to LogReg (16 configurations), RBF-SVM (80), HistGradientBoosting (320) and MLP (12) with the DESIGN §4 grids. (4) Shadow-kernel SVM (2106.12627 eq. 6): gamma in {0.5,1,3} x tau in {0.3,1,3} x C (8 values) x class_weight (2) = 144 configurations, with T capped at 256 snapshots for the kernel. (B) (1) The paper's single-shot NN on Pauli-6 outcomes, re-tuned (a replication check). (2) The shadow-kernel SVM as above. (3) Multi-shot nonlinear features: per-qubit same-basis collision rate (estimates single-qubit purity), 2-qubit purities, estimated stabiliser and Wilson-loop expectations, fed to tuned LogReg and GBT. (4) The non-learned collision-rate test with threshold 7/12.
- **Access model:** Every learner receives copies of each state one at a time, with the same number S of copies per test state and the same labelled training states with the same copies per training state (1024). Quantum learners may apply, to each single copy, a circuit of depth O(log n) built from 2-3-qubit gates (QCNN pooling with mid-circuit measurement and feed-forward allowed) before measuring. They have no quantum memory across copies and no ancillas. Classical learners make only single-qubit measurements in Pauli bases, random or fixed, on each copy; any classical processing across shots and across training states is allowed. The only access difference is entangling processing inside one copy before measurement. That is weaker than the quantum-memory separation of M8 and is reported as such. QCNN and HNN training at exact expectation values is a stated favour to the quantum side; if a quantum learner wins, it is re-run with shot-based training whose copies are counted. Every copy used in training and in test is reported for every learner.
- **Metric:** Balanced accuracy on held-out test states (new couplings, new product states) against copies per test state: S in {1,4,16,64,256} for A and S in {1,3,7,13,30,100} for B. Primary budgets: S_A*=16 and S_B*=7 (B's is the HNN's published inference budget). Secondary: S90 and S99, the smallest S on the grid reaching 0.90 and 0.99, with bootstrap CIs. Total training copies are reported per learner.
- **Success rule:** For each dataset, the best quantum learner beats the best classical learner (chosen by grouped CV on training data only, never by test score) if its balanced accuracy at the primary budget is higher on >=4 of 5 seeds AND the mean paired difference exceeds the SD of the paired differences (DESIGN §6). The same rule with roles swapped decides classical wins; anything else is 'no clear difference'. Validity conditions, checked before the main comparison is read: (i) the re-implemented published comparisons must reproduce their direction. In A the exact QCNN must need fewer copies than the single SOP near the boundary; in B the HNN must beat the re-tuned linear single-shot Pauli-6 NN by >=3x in S99. Otherwise report 'replication failed' and do not interpret. (ii) Configuration counts are printed and the classical grid is >= the quantum grid.
- **Milestone:** M8 (learning from quantum data or quantum-enhanced experiments); secondarily M2 (whether quantum-generated data alone give an advantage).
- **Kill check:** Stage 0, exact computation only, under 30 CPU minutes, before any learner is coded. (A) From exact expectation values at n=9, compute Cong et al.'s sample complexity M_min (their eq. 4) for the exact d=1 QCNN and for SOPs of length 5 and 7 along the test line. If the SOP/QCNN M_min ratio is below 1.5 everywhere outside the excluded window, the claimed advantage does not exist at <=10 qubits: record a kill for A. (B) From exact distributions, compute the multi-shot error against S of the non-learned collision-rate test, and the HNN's S99 with the inverse-preparation circuit under the paper's averaging rule. If the collision test reaches 99% balanced accuracy at S <= 1.5x the HNN's S99, the quantum shot advantage for B is killed without training anything (the back-of-envelope estimate predicts this outcome). Also verify the surface-code ground state's single-qubit marginals are maximally mixed for h<=0.25, within 0.05.
- **Cost estimate:** About 1,100 new lines. src/qmlrc/phases.py (Hamiltonians, ED in the symmetric sector, couplings and labels, surface code and product states), about 180. src/qmlrc/qdata.py (copy-limited simulators: Pauli-6 snapshots, fixed product bases, circuit-then-measure sampling), about 200. src/qmlrc/qcnn.py (exact Cong circuit, constant-depth version, trainable QCNN, HNN in PennyLane/JAX), about 250. Shadow kernel and multi-shot features, about 150. Experiment wiring with the paired rule and S90/S99, about 150. Tests, about 150. CPU: ED of fewer than 300 states of dimension <=1024 takes under 1 min. Fewer than 1e6 snapshots per seed. The shadow kernel with T<=256 and 40-80 training states takes about 1 min. GBT with 320 configurations takes under 1 min. QCNN/HNN training with exact distributions takes 2-4 min. About 6-8 min per seed per dataset, so about 1-1.5 h in total, split into experiments of <=10 min (one per dataset and size). Stage 0 alone is about 200 lines and under 30 min.
- **Win teaches:** A copy-matched advantage, robust to tuned nonlinear multi-shot classical learners, of a single-copy entangling measurement for recognising a phase at 9 qubits. It would confirm 1810.03787 / 2307.03711 or 2606.28199 under fair rules and justify a scaling study on hardware (M8). It would still be a constant-factor statement at fixed n, with no computational advantage: everything is simulable, and phase recognition is hard for quantum learners too at large correlation range (2510.08503).
- **Loss teaches:** The published copy advantages at this size come from weak classical baselines: a single string order parameter in A, a model linear in rho in B. Phase recognition at <=10 qubits gives no quantum-data advantage for single-copy coherent learners, which supports 2408.12739 and 2504.04101 under the repository's paired rule. Together with the scratch pilot below (no non-locally-easy SPT dataset with a surviving two-copy invariant at n=10), M8 progress in this theme then needs either larger systems or genuinely multi-copy tasks, not phase recognition at 10 qubits.
- **Closest prior work:** arXiv:1810.03787 (Original QCNN claim of a copy advantage over single SOPs (N=135).); arXiv:2307.03711 (Constant-depth QCNN; exponential copy reduction over local Pauli estimation of the multiscale SOP.); arXiv:2606.28199 (HNN: about 10x fewer shots than a classical NN on Pauli-6 data for the surface-code phase, N=9-25; classical baseline linear in rho.); arXiv:2408.12739 (Phase datasets are locally easy; a classical shadow surrogate matches QCNNs.); arXiv:2504.04101 (Local partial-tomography NP test beats QCNN and exact QCNN at equal copies.); arXiv:2109.05909 (7-qubit hardware QCNN against direct SOP measurement.); arXiv:2106.12627 (Shadow-kernel phase classification theorem (conditional on few-body separability).); arXiv:2510.08503 (Hardness of phase recognition exponential in correlation range, for quantum learners too.)

**Adversarial review: KILLED.** KILLED as designed, though its core can be salvaged as a cheap exact note. The scripts for all numbers are in the scratchpad (/tmp/claude-0/-home-user-quantum-ml-reality-check/3242e8f8-b193-546c-b381-3fe888e7979d/scratchpad/round01/t7_review/): b_surface.py, b_compare.py, a_cluster.py, a_cross.py. They are indicative, not repository results. (A) Cong's exact QCNN is not a coherent-measurement advantage. 2109.05909 (full-text appendix) already shows its output can be sampled directly from two product bases at at most 2x the copies, at any depth. The exponential-basis claim of 2307.03711 belongs to a different QCNN and says nothing for n <= 10. My exact n=9 computation gives the same per-copy Chernoff information (0.99-1.00x) for the CZ+X measurement underlying every QCNN in 1810.03787 and 2307.03711 as for the best fixed product Pauli basis. The only remaining room is the generic factor of about 2 from pure-state one-sided tests. The proposal's stage-0 check for A compares against a single SOP and would spuriously pass. Its labels use the iDMRG critical point 0.423, while the n=9 crossover is at about 0.19-0.20. Cong's sign-threshold M_min cannot label the paramagnetic side at n=9 because all SOPs stay positive. (B) The field commutes with the Z plaquettes. A fixed Z-basis parity check has zero false negatives and a false-positive rate of 7.4e-5 at S=4, beating the published HNN budgets. It does not even detect topological order. The ideal HNN keeps only an S99 of 1 vs 2 (about 1x at 1e-4), a known stabilizer-counting constant. The proposal's collision-test numbers come from a wrong independence assumption (actual 0.84/0.95/0.98, not 0.91/0.995/0.99997), and its B kill check compares against an HNN whose S99 is 1, so it would not kill B. Its marginal check fails (mean <Z> up to 0.44). Both arms are already published, B also on hardware (2606.28201). Recommendation: do not build the learner study. If anything, record a ~200-line exact 'ceiling' statement: at <= 10 qubits, single-copy entangling measurements give at most a constant factor of about 2 over the best product-basis learner on these phase tasks. Cite it as consolidating 2109.05909, 2504.04101 and 2606.28199/28201. Search budget: 3 arxiv_search.py calls; 3 arXiv id_list requests (1 returned 503 and was retried after more than 30 s). Every cited ID was resolved in this task through the arXiv API.

- *Already done:* Both arms are published, and the full text says more than the proposal credits. Arm A: Cong et al. (1810.03787) already compare the exact QCNN with single string order parameters (SOPs) via M_min (eq. 4). Their own inset gives an SOP/QCNN ratio of about 1.73e^{0.28d}, roughly 2.3 at depth d=1. Herrmann et al. (2109.05909; I read pp. 1-4 and 12-14 of the full text) ran a 7-qubit hardware QCNN against direct SOP measurement. Their appendix shows that every product of SOPs in the output of Cong's QCNN has X on one sublattice and Z or identity on the other. So 'the output of the QCNN proposed in Ref. [11] can be efficiently sampled directly from the input state' with two product bases, and this 'requires only twice as many measurements' at ANY depth. The exponential number of bases in 2307.03711 (I read pp. 1-4, 10, 12 and 22) applies to their error-tolerant QCNN with Z-error correction, and its lower bound 3^{3^{d-4}} says nothing for d <= 3 (n <= 10 means d <= 2). On equal copies, 2504.04101 (abstract) already reports a local-tomography Neyman-Pearson test that beats the order-parameter classifier, the QCNN and shadow-based ML. 2408.12739 (abstract) already simulates QCNN phase classification classically from Pauli shadows. Arm B: the surface-code-vs-1-design task, with a Pauli-6 NN baseline, was run at N=9-25 in 2606.28199 (I read pp. 1, 4-8, 11 and 15). It was also run on superconducting hardware at 3x3 and 4x4 in 2606.28201 (I read pp. 1-2, 4-7 and 14-15), reaching 99% accuracy with 10 shots. That paper also notes that the Z stabilizers do not change with the field because they commute with it. What the proposal would add is tuned baselines and the paired rule. But the answer it would produce can already be computed analytically (see below), so as designed it adds nothing that still needs testing.
- *Classical can match:* Yes, on both arms, by methods the proposal leaves out or mis-specifies. (A) The proposal forgets a single product basis (X on one sublattice, Z on the other) read with Cong's exact Boolean function or a nonlinear classifier on the raw bits. That learner reproduces the exact Cong QCNN at most a factor 2 off, and exactly for a single-ZXZ readout (2109.05909 App.; it also follows from eqs. 8-9 of 1810.03787). Baseline (2) splits copies over 2-3 bases, and the Pauli-6 shadows waste copies (a random shot hits the 9-site basis with probability 3^-9). Both are handicaps that could produce a spurious quantum win. I ran an exact check at n=9 (scratch a_cluster.py; OBC chain, P_odd=P_even=+1 sector, h1=0.5). I took four pairs of ground states straddling the transition. The per-copy classical Chernoff information of the CZ-disentangler + X-basis measurement (all ZXZ stabilizers on both sublattices, the quantum part of every QCNN in 1810.03787 and 2307.03711) is 0.99-1.00 times that of the best of all 3^9 fixed product Pauli bases; plain all-X is just as good. By data processing, no Boolean post-processing can exceed this. The only remaining room is the pure-state ceiling -log|<psi|phi>|^2, which is 1.96-1.99 times the best product basis. That factor of about 2 is generic for any pair of close pure states, because a projector onto one hypothesis gives a one-sided test. It is not a phase-recognition effect, and it is not available for composite classes. (B) The field is along Z and commutes with the Z plaquettes, so <B_p> = 1 exactly at every h (exact diagonalisation, scratch b_surface.py). A non-learned test in the Z basis (accept iff all 4 Z plaquettes are +1 on every shot) therefore has zero false negatives. Its false-positive rate on 1-design product states is exactly 1/16, 5.1e-3, 5.1e-4 and 7.4e-5 at S=1-4, flooring at 3.2e-6. It reaches the paper's <0.01% target at S=4, against the HNN's published S=7 (N=25) and S=30 (N=9). The ideal HNN (inverse preparation circuit, i.e. the joint eigenbasis of all 8 stabilizers plus Z_L, with the paper's mean-of-shots rule) has balanced accuracy 0.9935 at S=1 for h<=0.15, so its S99 is 1. The Z-basis test needs S=2. At the 1e-4 level both need 4-5 shots. The residual gap of at most 2x comes from measuring commuting X- and Z-type stabilizers in one shot. That is a known constant factor (1709.03353 abstract: local, non-adaptive, non-collective strategies cost only a constant factor), and it does not detect topology. The Z-basis test also accepts trivial ground states beyond h_c, because B_p=+1 holds in both phases. So task B is closer to stabilizer-state verification against random product states than to phase recognition.
- *Access model issue:* The access model is stated clearly: copies come one at a time; the quantum side may apply a log-depth entangling circuit to each copy; the classical side makes single-qubit Pauli measurements. Comparing single-copy entangling measurements with product measurements is physically meaningful, but it is weaker than the quantum-memory separation of M8, and the proposal says so. Problems: (1) The classical side is restricted to Pauli bases, and adaptivity across copies is not specified. A fair measure-first learner should be allowed any local basis and adaptive basis choice. (2) Training is asymmetric: quantum models are trained on exact expectations (infinitely many copies), classical ones on 1024 copies per state. That favours the quantum side, which is acceptable only because a quantum win would be re-run, so the primary comparison cannot be read until then. (3) The real access difference in B is that one coherent shot reads 8 commuting stabilizers while one product shot reads 4. For certification-type tasks, the literature puts local strategies within a constant factor (1709.03353 abstract), so this access model can only ever give a fixed-n constant factor, not an M8 separation. (4) The B baseline taken from the paper (a random-Pauli NN, linear in rho) handicaps the classical side arbitrarily. A fixed Z-basis measurement is equally 'classical' and removes most of the gap.
- *Feasibility:* Compute is not the problem. My scratch checks ran in minutes on CPU: exact diagonalisation of the 3x3 surface code and the exact false-positive rate of the Z-basis test in about 3 min, and Chernoff information over all 3^9 product bases at n=9 in about 1 min. Decisiveness is the problem. (i) The exact-QCNN arm of A is decided analytically (at most 2x, or 1x, by 2109.05909), and my Chernoff computation already shows that the constant-depth QCNN measurement equals the best product basis per copy at n=9. (ii) The stage-0 kill checks as written would wrongly let both arms survive. A's check compares the QCNN with a single SOP, the weak baseline the proposal says it fixes, and Cong's own d=1 ratio is about 2.3 > 1.5. B's check compares the collision test with the ideal HNN's S99, which is 1, so 'collision S99 <= 1.5 x HNN S99' can never hold. The proposal's prediction that stage 0 kills B is therefore inverted. (iii) Cong's sign-threshold M_min (p0=0.5) is ill-defined for the paramagnetic test states at n=9: all SOPs stay positive along the whole line (S5/S7/S9 = 0.19/0.09/0.08 at h2=0.6 and 0.075/0.026/0.019 at h2=1.0). (iv) At n=9 the finite-size crossover sits at h2 of about 0.19-0.20 (d2E/dh2 minimum and fidelity-susceptibility peak), not at 0.423, so the iDMRG labels and the exclusion window |h2-0.423|<0.1 misclassify states with h2 in (0.2, 0.32). (v) B's S grid {1,3,7,...} cannot resolve the real gap of S99 = 1 vs 2. (vi) About 1,100 lines of code would mostly reproduce results that a 200-line exact computation settles.
- *Technical errors:* Mechanism attributes the exponential number of product bases (2307.03711) to Cong's exact QCNN. 2109.05909 App. (full text) shows that Cong's QCNN output needs only two sublattice product bases at any depth, i.e. at most 2x the measurements. The 2307.03711 bound 3^{3^{d-4}} concerns the error-tolerant QCNN and says nothing at n <= 10 (d <= 2).; Stage-0 kill check A compares the QCNN's M_min with single SOPs of length 5 and 7, the weak baseline. Cong's own inset (1.73e^{0.28d}, about 2.3 at d=1) suggests it passes the 1.5 threshold and spuriously keeps A alive, although a single-basis classical learner matches the QCNN to within 2x.; Cong's M_min (sign test p > 0.5) cannot label paramagnetic states at n=9: S5/S7/S9 > 0 along the whole test line h1=0.5 (e.g. 0.075/0.026/0.019 at h2=1.0; exact diagonalisation in the symmetric sector).; The n=9 finite-size crossover on h1=0.5 is at h2 of about 0.19-0.20 (d2E/dh2 minimum at 0.20, fidelity-susceptibility peak at 0.19), not 0.423. The proposed exclusion window [0.32, 0.52] misses it, and 'a window fixed from the exact SOP curve' is ill-defined because that curve is smooth.; B validity check 'single-qubit marginals maximally mixed within 0.05 for h <= 0.25' is false. The field is along Z: mean <Z_i> = 0.07, 0.15, 0.25, 0.35, 0.44 at h = 0.05-0.25 (max 0.58).; The collision-test numbers (0.91, 0.995, 0.99997 at S=7, 13, 20) come from treating all C(S,2) same-basis pairs as independent; I reproduce 0.912/0.995/0.99998 under that assumption. A Monte Carlo with the true magnetised states gives about 0.84, 0.95 and 0.98 (h in [0, 0.25]).; Kill check B compares the collision test with the HNN's S99 under the inverse-preparation circuit. That S99 is 1 (balanced accuracy 0.9935 at S=1 for h <= 0.15), so the criterion cannot be met and stage 0 would not kill B. The proposal's expected outcome is reversed.; Published N=9 budgets S_inf=30 (HNN) and 200 (Pauli-6) in 2606.28199 Fig. 5 were chosen large so that errors come from training. They are not minimal inference budgets. The primary budget S_B*=7 comes from N=25 (Fig. 3), not N=9.; B's topological class h in [0, 0.25] at N=9 goes past the finite-size transition (h_c = 0.17 in 2606.28199 App. B; 0.18 in my exact diagonalisation). And because B_p = +1 in both phases, task B does not test topological order at all.
- *Required fixes:* Drop the 1,100-line learner study and replace it with a roughly 200-line exact 'ceiling' computation, a precise numerical statement that passes K6. For each task at n <= 10, compute per-copy distinguishability (exact Bayes error vs S, or Chernoff information) for: the best fixed product Pauli basis (all 3^n), the sublattice bases, the CZ-disentangler + X-basis measurement (by data processing, an upper bound for every QCNN of 1810.03787 and 2307.03711), and the quantum ceiling. Kill the quantum claim if the QCNN-type measurement is below 1.5x the best product basis. My scratch result for 4 pairs at n=9 is 0.99-1.00x, so A is already killed.; Compute the quantum ceiling for composite classes, not for pairs of pure states. Use for example the Helstrom/Chernoff bound between class mixtures, or the worst case over the class. The pairwise -log F ceiling (about 2x here) is a generic one-sided-test factor for any two close pure states and must not be reported as a phase-recognition advantage.; Add the missing classical baseline to A: a single sublattice product basis (copies not split) read with Cong's exact Boolean function, or with GBT/MLP on the raw bits. Report it as equal to Cong's QCNN within 2x by 2109.05909 App.; In A, define labels from the n=9 crossover (h2 of about 0.19-0.20) or exclude [0.05, 0.45] on h1=0.5. Use learned thresholds instead of Cong's p0=0.5 sign rule.; In B, replace the collision test with the Z-basis stabilizer-parity test (zero false negatives exactly; false-positive rate 1/16, 5.1e-3, 5.1e-4, 7.4e-5 at S=1-4) and with the exact ideal HNN (joint stabilizer eigenbasis). Report the residual gap honestly: S99 is 1 vs 2 at the 99% level and about 1x at 1e-4. Its cause is simultaneous measurement of commuting X/Z stabilizers, a known constant factor (1709.03353), not topology.; Restrict B's topological class to h <= 0.12-0.15 at N=9. Remove the maximally-mixed-marginal check. State that B does not test topological order unless the trivial class also satisfies B_p=+1 (e.g. paramagnet ground states h > h_c), and that this case is locally easy through magnetisation, as 2606.28199 itself notes.; Use the published HNN numbers correctly (S_inf=30/200 at N=9 are training-limited, not inference budgets; S=7 is N=25) and use a fine S grid (1, 2, 3, 4, 5, ...) at small S.; If any learner comparison is kept, allow the classical side arbitrary local bases and adaptive basis choice, count training copies symmetrically, and label the arm 'replication of known results (2109.05909 App.; 2504.04101; 2606.28199/28201)'.
- *Further prior work:* arXiv:2109.05909 (Already cited, but its key content was missed. Full text pp. 12-14: every product of SOPs in the output of Cong's QCNN has X on one sublattice and Z/I on the other. Direct sampling needs only 2 product bases and at most 2x the measurements at any depth. Their modified FC layer needs 3 bases at d=1, N=7. 7-qubit hardware vs direct SOP measurement.); arXiv:2606.28201 (Scarato, Knorzer, Hoffmann et al. Full text pp. 1-2, 4-7, 14-15. Hardware run of the HNN on the 3x3 and 4x4 surface code in a Z field against random Pauli-product states: >85% single-shot, 99% at 10 shots. Notes that the Z stabilizers do not decay with the field because they commute with it, which is the basis of the trivial classical Z-parity test. So arm B is already done on hardware.); arXiv:2209.12428 (Cong, Maskara, Tran, Pichler, Semeghini et al., 'Enhancing Detection of Topological Order by Local Error Correction'. Abstract only: local error-corrected decoration for robust detection of toric-code order, applied to Rydberg-simulator data. Relevant as a non-QCNN route to sharper topological diagnostics; I did not verify whether it works purely from snapshots.); arXiv:1709.03353 (Pallister, Linden, Montanaro, 'Optimal verification of entangled states with local measurements'. Abstract: efficient verification protocols for any stabilizer state. Restricting to local, non-adaptive, non-collective measurements costs only a constant factor. Supports the at-most-2x ceiling for arm B, which is in effect stabilizer-state verification against product states.); arXiv:1104.4695 (Flammia and Liu, 'Direct Fidelity Estimation from Few Pauli Measurements'. Title and authors only (not read). A candidate classical certification baseline for B.); arXiv:2201.05957 (Gong et al., 61-qubit 'quantum neuronal sensing'. Abstract read: classifies ergodic vs localized phases on hardware by measuring one qubit. Checked and NOT an SPT/topological comparison; tangential prior hardware work on quantum-processing quantum-state data.)
